(fi) mr president, my colleague, mr joke swiebel, has chosen a demanding method of working for his report. he takes aim at the countries mentioned directly, blaming them for their lack of human rights. the method gives concrete evidence of the human rights situation in europe, but places a great responsibility on its user to ensure that the picture given is correct. unfortunately, this has not been entirely successful. it is clear from paragraph 27 that there is no functioning civil service system in finland. it calls on finland and greece to unconditionally recognise the right to conscientious objection to military service without reference to any religious grounds. however, the right to civil service has existed in finland since 1931. under the law now in force, virtually every conscript can choose to serve in the civilian service. some 2 500 people, or 7% of the age group, exercise this right each year. about 80 percent serve in the military, and a little over 10 percent are exempt. the concern expressed in the report is apparently that the length of civilian service in finland is twice as long as the shortest service. care is also taken of the many twenty total rejects in prison who do not want to fulfill their civic duty in any way. the longer service period for civil servants in finland is justified by the fact that their duty is eight hours a day within the normal working week when military service is 24 hours a day. i think it is still up to the finnish parliament to weigh up the matter. the fact that the vast majority of finnish young men join the army is not due to a lack of alternative civilian service. the importance of conscription for the country's independence is still well remembered in finland. after all, during world war ii, helsinki was the only capital of a belligerent country, along with london and moscow, that was not occupied.
(fi) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by thanking all the rapporteurs for the railway package on behalf of my group. they've done a really good job. but the railroad was not started until too late. now, among other things, the promotion of competition in both freight and passenger transport, the increase of interoperability and the improvement of safety are huge challenges that require really fast action. the railways have lost much of their competitiveness. transport has shifted to busy roads and airplanes because the railways have not been able to improve their services fast enough. this railway package will help to make european railways more competitive, and improving competitiveness will enable businesses and citizens to use a more environmentally friendly form of transport. at the same time, congestion can be reduced and short flights replaced by rail transport, and a better balance between different modes of transport can be achieved. the development of railways also opens up the possibility of increasing the use of intermodal transport, while improving the interoperability of ports and railways. i have been following the preparation of mr savary's report as a shadow rapporteur. mr. savary has done a good job, too. when properly implemented, the agency shall promote the development of railways, monitor and act as a catalyst for new innovations and good practice. however, our group believes that the same principle should be followed in the formation of the agency's management board as in the maritime safety agency. we have tabled an amendment to this, which we hope will be supported.

(fi) mr president, commissioner, clearing is an essential part of all transactions in the securities markets. the commission communication on clearing and settlement in the european union analyses the reasons for the inefficiency of crossborder clearing and settlement. the inefficiency is largely due to technical requirements and market practices, as well as fragmentation due to national differences and legal barriers. it is very important that the authorities responsible for supervision follow a common approach and harmonised rules. the first step should be to remove technical barriers and move towards interoperability of systems. the preparation of single market directives understandably often starts with the situation of large member states, which sometimes seems to lead to the application of the 'one size fits all' principle. the solution chosen in the preparation of eu securities legislation does not necessarily reflect the european single financial market, but rather the application of the national solution on a european scale. this principle is very poorly suited to the stock market, which will for a long time be characterized by national characteristics. i believe that this is also partly the case in the report by mr generoso andria which we are discussing today. paragraphs 11 and 12 of the report set out the most desirable way of organising securities clearing and the relationship between the csds and the depositaries. in summary, the message of those paragraphs is that clearing activities should define the core activities that csds and clearing houses should carry out for profit and under the control of users, while separating the providers of services. i am sorry to say that i cannot agree with the proposal for a solution put forward by our colleague on these two points. the clearing and custody of securities is much more diverse between member states than the securities exchange. for example, in germany, physical securities are still stored in vaults, whereas in finland, securities have been converted entirely into electronic records, into securities. the german shareholder's position differs substantially from that of the finnish shareholder in company transactions, for example, because of the national company law. the fundamental difference between the structure of the continental and nordic csds is that in the nordic csd, the information on the investor is entered directly into the csd system and, except in denmark, also directly into the issuer's shareholder register. in continental european schemes, custody management is recorded and the investor has no direct contact with the issuer. the commission has also been aware of these differences and is approaching the investigation with caution and from a functional point of view, which is very good. if a directive is adopted on this subject, the commission's approach, which takes account of these market differences, should be maintained. for example, in my home country of finland, finnish issuers have valued value-added services based on the shareholder register in their day-to-day operations; for example, the large company nokia considers them to be very important. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one. i have prepared two amendments with my colleague astrid lulling, which aim to maintain the efficiency of the national markets and to allow all structural systems and alternatives to exist. there is an english proverb that says: "if it ain't broken, don't fix it". the rapporteur, mr andria, is trying to remedy a situation which is working. in this connection, i would stress that these two amendments should be adopted and i hope that my group will support them.

(fi) mr president, the concept of the northern dimension must be understood in a broad sense. it covers both the union's external relations and its internal policies. in external relations, it covers the baltic and barents sea regions and the whole arctic region. as the european union expands, the importance of crossborder cooperation will increase, as our common border with russia grows and as we gain new neighbours. crossborder cooperation is primarily a bilateral activity, between the eu and its member states on the one hand and russia and our other neighbours on the other. on the other hand, we also have two useful multilateral fora, the council of the baltic sea states and the barents euro-arctic council. both bilateral and multilateral activities are limited by a lack of funds. in bilateral crossborder cooperation, eu funding is limited to interreg projects and a small number of tacis projects. the barents and baltic sea cooperation is not funded by the union at all. our joint resolution again proposes the creation of a specific budget line for the northern dimension. in practice, this could mean a substantial increase in eu funding for the baltic sea council and barents council area, channelled at least partly through these councils. this time we will be dealing with the northern dimension on the basis of a statement by the commissioner responsible for external relations. the principles of the northern dimension must also be borne in mind when we consider the regional and agricultural policies of the enlarged union and other areas of internal policies which have a particular impact on the development of the northern regions. the northern dimension will also become more important for internal policies with the accession of the baltic states and poland to the union.

commission. - madam president, first of all i would like to thank you on behalf of the commission for inviting us to make a statement on this subject. the commission welcomes the opportunity offered by parliament to reiterate the importance which the community attaches to the conservation and protection of biodiversity in the marine environment. this importance was already highlighted by parliament and the council in the sixth community environment programme, which aims, among other things, to achieve no further loss of biodiversity by 2010. the protection and conservation of wild fauna and flora is also one of the objectives defined in the european commission's communication 'towards a strategy for the protection and conservation of the marine environment'. cetaceans are fragile members of the marine fauna and are threatened by a range of human activities, although it is often not easy to link these activities to the situation of these animals. the commission is aware of the current debate on the possible impact of the use of active low frequency sonar on some cetacean species. it is possible that the emission of these low frequency, intense tones disturbs or alters cetaceans, but this matter needs to be investigated in more detail to determine its severity and extent. the lack of certainty about the reality of these impacts does not in any case justify not taking action to address this issue. the commission considers that, given the potential impact on the marine environment, this type of technology should be used with extreme caution and ideally its potential impact should be assessed beforehand. let me remind you that cetaceans are protected by directive 92/43, the habitats directive, which obliges member states to take the necessary measures to prevent, inter alia, deliberate harassment of these species. for these reasons and following the recent events in the canary islands, the commission asked the national authorities of the member states where such events may have occurred for more details on this subject at the meeting of the habitats committee on 20 november. we will now send a reminder to the member states so that they do not forget our request. the commission shall forward to parliament all information obtained from this initiative. the commission will also ask independent scientific bodies, such as the international council for the exploration of the sea, to analyse current developments and the knowledge available on this subject, in order to obtain more information on the impact that the use of active low frequency sonar may have on the marine environment. only with sound scientific information will we be able to determine what kind of additional measures to take on top of those already in place.
(fi) mr president, i would like to thank the council of presidents for taking up this important matter and i would like to thank the honourable member for his reply. first of all, it is perfectly legitimate to seek to develop a defence system to monitor new technology submarines which are difficult to detect with traditional passive probes. it is clear that submarines invisible to radar are only used by those with hostile intentions. that's why it's important to keep up with the news. the question is what technology we choose and what its ecological cost is. the low-frequency radar system, or lfas, developed by nato countries, may cause whale strays to beaches and fatal internal damage to their internal organs. the high-sibel, low-frequency sound produced by the radar can travel thousands of miles out to sea. whale deaths have occurred in areas where the united states has tested the system. in march 2000, 17 whales washed ashore in the bahamas, and eight of them died while testing a naval radar system at sea. autopsies on dead whales revealed blood spills in the brain and inner ears caused by extremely loud noise. the us navy itself stated in its report on the whale killings in the bahamas that lfas was the most likely cause of the whale killings. in september 2002, the canary islands, in the eu, suffered the worst whale deaths in 20 years, when 15 whales of different species died from internal bleeding. at the same time, nato was conducting military exercises in the area involving 58 warships and six submarines from various member states of the alliance. the lfas produces 1,000 hertz of low frequency and an average of 250 decibels of sound, scraping the seabed from hundreds of miles away. the effects of loud but low-frequency sound on the whale's behaviour and internal organs are varied. noise can cause damage to the earlobes and bleeding in the brain and lungs. even farther away from the source of the sound, the system may confuse the whale's communication system by operating at the same low frequencies that the whales themselves use. noise makes it difficult for whales to navigate, form herds, mate, and feed. the link between the radar and whale deaths is challenging and difficult to prove, as tracking whales and studying changes in their behaviour over the long term is very difficult. the u.s. navy has conducted an environmental impact assessment of the system, but reputable american environmental organizations consider it flawed. last november, a federal judge barred the navy from testing the system in u.s. territorial waters for the time being, citing marine mammal protection laws. however, the navy has permission from the national authority to test the system for 432 hours in other sea areas until august of this year. so far, the navy has agreed to test its equipment away from coastal areas and known breeding or transit areas for marine mammals. however, scientists have found that testing is not safe even in the middle of the pacific ocean, since it is impossible to know the whale's paths completely. by arranging it, up to 75 percent of the world's oceans could be covered. it is not fully understood how the system will work in the mediterranean or baltic sea, where the radar range is coast to coast and animals have no opportunity to move out of the sound zone. the eu has a clear obligation to act and to demand information about the system. there is also a clear economic interest: fishermen operating in the eu have been concerned over the past year about the impact of the radar system on the stocks in their fishing waters. even if fish are less sensitive to the adverse effects of the system than sea turtles, it is clear that the noise of the radar may scare fish away and thus hinder the practice of the profession. mr president, i thank the commission for its statement. on the basis of the above, efforts must be made to obtain a temporary ban on the radar system and to establish rapidly the effects of the system on the marine fauna. it would be best if, on the basis of the commission statement heard today and on the basis of information obtained elsewhere, the environment committee of the european parliament took up the matter and drew up a report.
(fi) mr president, geneva is a moment of truth for us. it is again a test of our ability or inability to stand united in defence of democracy, the rule of law and human rights in a world where the human rights situation has deteriorated significantly and the general political climate is worryingly cooling. the intensification of the crisis in iraq led the member states to introduce the article 19 teu procedure in the un security council. in the long term, it is even more important to intensify actively cooperation on human rights under articles 19 and 20 both in the un and in other international fora. we can't do this if we lose our chance to present ourselves as one. we have no credible front and we, the old europe, are left without the moral authority we need. we cannot then expect our own citizens and ngos to take us seriously either.

the rapporteur. (fi) mr president, the proposal now before us is the second of three directives aimed at bringing the legislation of the member states into line with the aarhus convention. as with the first directive on access to environmental information, parliament's third reading has now seen the adoption of a compromise text which is very representative of parliament's position and which fully meets and even exceeds the minimum requirement laid down in the aarhus treaty. the council already accepted in its common position many elements of parliament's first reading, just as parliament could accept many elements of the common position. the number of problems thus raised for conciliation was considerably smaller than for the first pillar. on the other hand, these few questions were associated with major problems, due to differences in approach. the commission proposal and the council's common position were based on the so-called 'quick fix' principle, whereas parliament had hoped for a broader, horizontal approach. however, it is clear that parliament's aim is not to defend its position verbatim, but to ensure that there are no grey areas in eu legislation where public participation would be justified but is not possible due to the clumsiness of the legislator. in mediation, we may have found a two-step solution to the problem. on the other hand, the requirements for public participation, based on the aarhus convention, are being incorporated into the new community legislation at an early stage. on the other hand, the existing legislation and the scope of the directive will be reviewed in the light of the experience gained during the first six years of application. the member states undertook to promote practical opportunities for public participation in the mediation. the authorities are encouraged, inter alia, to inform the public about the decisions taken, the reasons and considerations underlying them and the public participation process. the time limits for the different stages of participation in decision-making shall be reasonable to allow the public to prepare and participate effectively in decision-making. in addition, public authorities are encouraged to promote public education on environmental issues, which is undoubtedly also in the public authorities' own interest, as it can improve the quality of participation. a specific question concerned public participation in the verification of permits. in this case, the parties to the mediation sought to strike a balance. on the other hand, the public can participate genuinely when there is a real need to review the authorizations of plants and factories, i.e. when there is a risk of increasing environmental damage. on the other hand, the authority may review licences without the participation of the public when implementing acts and regulations where there is no genuine discretion. i think we have succeeded in this: the public can participate when decisions are actually being made about the environment, but a valuable instrument of participation is not inflated by asking the public to participate in routine decisions. the adoption of the procedure for derogations for defence projects, which was probably a small victory for parliament in its field of application but important in principle, was a victory for parliament in the conciliation. in accordance with the aarhus convention, projects relating to national defence can be excluded from the scope of the directive only on the basis of a case-by-case assessment by the authorities of the member states. after the decision-making process that follows public participation, the public must naturally be able to evaluate the decisions taken. the mediation process resulted in a solution to parliament's satisfaction, which lays down the grounds for granting or refusing access to information to the public and obliges the public authorities to provide the public with practical information on the possibility of resorting, if necessary, to administrative and judicial redress. in particular, in order to ensure a conciliation result, the commission has undertaken in its statement annexed to the directive to submit a proposal for a directive in the first quarter of 2003 on the implementation of the aarhus convention in environmental matters as regards the right of appeal and the right to bring proceedings. it is therefore possible to have confidence that the whole process of ratification of the aarhus convention will proceed in the community without undue delay. i thank the commission and the council, and in particular denmark, which has already ceded the presidency to greece, for their good cooperation in the conciliation and in the informal negotiations that preceded it. i warmly recommend that parliament adopt the result of the conciliation at tomorrow's vote.
(fi) mr president, commissioner, the result of the conciliation is good and worthy of support. the aim of the directive is to bring community legislation into line with the aarhus treaty. it has already been stated here that the directive gives the public a full right to participate in the decision-making process from the outset, in particular in the planning and implementation of programmes, the environmental impact assessment process and pollution prevention and reduction in general. it also allows decision-makers to take the views and concerns of the public into account when making decisions. this naturally and rightly increases the transparency of the decision-making process and helps to increase public interest and awareness of environmental issues. in the light of the main results of the conciliation, the requirements for public participation under the aarhus convention will be incorporated into the relevant community legislation at an early stage in the future. it is also important that the authorities also make the grounds for decisions more widely known. the important thing is that in general, many of the paragraphs require clear language and that the authorities help the public to understand the information available. it is also good that public participation is increased on the most important issues, that the criteria for granting or refusing access to information to the public are strengthened and, once again, that the public authorities are obliged to provide correct, clear and unambiguous information. i think that on this basis we can expect that the public will be more inclined to take action on environmental matters.
the rapporteur. (fi) mr president, i am here to present the final evaluation of the fuel quality directive which we originally adopted in this house in 1998. the european parliament then passed the world's strictest quality standards for transport fuels, after a heated debate and decision-making. in particular, we argued with the oil industry about the need to reduce the sulphur content of gasoline and diesel fuel. we had to make a huge number of arguments to get the reform through, which reduced the sulphur content of diesel and petrol considerably to a lower level than at that time. this choice proved to be very strategic and wise. indeed, reducing the sulphur content of fuels has been found to be perhaps even more important in reducing air pollution. now, this reform that we are finalising today in parliament is that by 2009 only petrol and diesel fuel with no sulphur at all, practically no sulphur at all, may be sold in the european union. this is very important if we are to really clean up the exhaust emissions of cars even further. but the more important reason for this reform is now that car manufacturers are constantly developing cleaner engines with ever lower emissions. these new engines, such as direct injection engines, are in dire need of these unimpaired fuels. we can be satisfied with the outcome. we got the commission and the council to agree that the clean fuels should be on the market by 2009; we brought that timetable forward by two years. i would suggest that this situation is already coming about, if anything, because several member states have realised that this process can be considerably speeded up by granting small tax breaks to cleaner fuels. i can perhaps say that sweden and finland were the countries where this was realised earlier and clean fuels were brought to the market very quickly. this directive will be reviewed in 2005. it will then be up to the commission to decide whether the diesel fuel rule will be implemented in 2009. as rapporteur, i regret only that we have not decided now that the non-violent diesel fuels will be on the market in 2009. i think that this mid-term review in 2005 will only complicate matters, but we were not able to get this through parliament in the second reading either. it is suspected that the production of unadulterated diesel fuel would be associated with increased co2 emissions. however, i think that we could have adopted this reform now, because clean diesel will again compensate for the accelerated entry of new vehicles on the market and thus reduce co2 emissions. the 2005 re-evaluation will also discuss how to bring non-flammable or low-flammable fuels onto the market, including for machine tools. this was perhaps the most difficult part of these mediation negotiations. i would say that i am relatively pleased with the outcome, which is that by 2009 clean fuels will also be used in tractors and other machines. i do not think that farmers will have to pay a very high additional price for this, because costs will generally come down in the end. mr president, i would like to correct one point in these motions for resolutions. they say that parliament adopted one amendment at first reading. this is not true. i would like to put it on the record that parliament adopted 36 amendments at first reading.

has in many places been to promote unilateral production in developing countries. at the same time, the export of heavily subsidised european agricultural products to developing countries is damaging for fair trade products, but has found that their high prices only decrease when demand increases. the eu should lower the threshold prices for fair trade products and raise the threshold prices for other developing countries' products. a reduction in the fairtrade threshold price would help to sell products within the eu. this would increase production volumes in developing countries. then there natural. the plenary must be informed of the compatibility of the financial framework with the financial perspective in order to be able to take a decision. the procedure increases the responsibility funding under rule 63a is therefore primarily in the interest of the committee responsible.

harmonisation from the point of view of consumer protection is that it allows a higher level of legislation to be enacted at national level, if that level cannot be reached in the european union. it (fi) mr president, i greatly appreciate and fully support the work of dr lies on this report, which sets out guidelines for the treatment of human tissues in the european union, also known as the community of values. it's a broad topic, so i'll just focus on one detail, the processing of stem cells. it is a direct continuation of the work we tried to do in the parliament's ad hoc committee on human genetics last year. we understood the cough: on the business side was the fire zone. investors were on the waiting list. expectations were not so much about new medical breakthroughs as, above all, about possible wealth. genetic engineering is the next economic boom, and it is expected to bring at least as much return on investment as it. in the end, it doesn't really matter what the research actually finds. while waiting for the jump, we had to completely ignore the fact that there is also an alternative to embryonic stem cell cloning, treatment with adult stem cells. however, embryonic stem cells have a greater ability to differentiate into any tissue. recently, however, research has opened up promising possibilities for the use of adult stem cells. the first cells in the human embryo are called totipotent, meaning that they can differentiate into any type of cell. pluripotent stem cells can again differentiate into any cell type, but they can no longer produce a new human. multipotent stem cells are no longer able to produce cells of a specific tissue, such as nerve cells, liver cells or blood cells. these are also found in adults, and they are the ones that are of most interest for therapeutic cloning. recent research suggests that some stem cells that were thought to be multipotent are actually pluripotent. this would mean that human embryos would not need to be made into stem cell lines after all. liese's report rightly acknowledges this situation, and he also acknowledges the ethical problems that arise in the handling of embryos. it is important to note here that medical goodness and ethical wisdom are not necessarily mutually exclusive. we, the christian democrats, say a strong yes to research, while at the same time wanting to protect the integrity of human dignity from its very beginning.

saccon. the feedback to date on environmental agreements has been that a significant proportion of environmental and civil society actors have been suspicious of voluntary agreements, even when the agreement itself has been voluntary but the agreement itself binding. on the other hand, the industry has been hoping to increase voluntary agreements to achieve flexibility and thus hopefully also positive environmental outcomes. this ditch, as far as i can see, is now well bridged. the report takes the view that voluntary agreements can be applied where the impact can be assessed in a uniform manner and the added value to society can be measured by sustainable development indicators. secondly, it must be able to define objectives and set clear timetables for them. in addition, the contract must be representative. in practice, this requirement means that sectors which are poorly organised or otherwise free to move do not fall within the scope of the voluntary agreement. voluntary agreements, like legislative acts, must take account of public participation and guarantee the transparency of the agreement. in addition, the binding nature of the contract, the evaluation of the results and any sanctions must be guaranteed. under the above conditions, there is no risk in using voluntary agreements. these conditions ensure that the agreement produces a better outcome for the environment and society than legislative instruments. i would also like to say that the amendment by mr oomen-ruijten must be supported. the contractual process generally generates a genuine commitment to the objective by the parties. often it is more important than the letter of the law or the contract. in this way, operators do not spend their resources on finding loopholes, but rather on finding ways to achieve the objective effectively.

(fi) mr president, the eldr group believes that the resolution agreed between the groups provides a good basis for the work of the eu-russia summit. the input is forward-looking and constructive, but also includes critical assessments. the resolution is also useful in view of the meeting of the parliamentary cooperation committee between the european parliament and the russian duma, which will be held in moscow next week. for my part, i would like to stress that we must strive to reduce economic and social disparities at all the external borders of the union. as the eu-russia border continues to widen, we need to develop crossborder cooperation vigorously. we must encourage the member states and their territories, particularly those on the eu's external borders, to establish direct cross-border links with russia. on the finnish-russian border, such systematic cooperation between neighbouring regions has been practised for more than a decade. similar action must now also be taken between estonia and russia on the one hand and latvia and russia on the other. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the establishment of a european union agency for the evaluation of medicinal products. the union must be prepared to finance this cooperation between neighbouring regions. a new financing system is needed for crossborder cooperation. the current tacis and interreg systems are not strong enough and have not been able to coordinate sufficiently. a new financing system must be created which can also be used at other external borders of the union. the phare system could be a model for this new system before it became part of the accession process.

commission. – mr president, mrs ferrer’s report provides very interesting ideas for developing the concept of capacity building and to describe the means to implement it. this is an area we have identified as a priority for our development cooperation. it is the main element in our overall policy statement dating back to november 2000, and adopted also by parliament. we are working to improve the strategic approach to european union action on capacity building and are approaching it from the perspective of good governance. the report is therefore timely, because it comes as we prepare the communication on governance in community development policy. this being said, capacity building is typically an area that has to be mainstreamed in all our interventions, and is increasingly recognised as a key factor in all areas of development. our communication will give priority attention to three specific topics – governance in general, political governance and economic governance – three themes which we see as part of the broader agenda of capacity building. i fully agree with the approach followed in mrs ferrer’s report on searching for a definition. particularly relevant are the need to adapt capacity building to the context and the rather broad notion of capacity building, which includes not only the 'classic' inputs such as 'training and materials', but also a range of innovative aspects. the reference to the need to avoid imposing foreign models is also highly welcome – ownership has to be promoted. the different priority sectors mentioned also show an innovative approach to institutional development; the security issue, in particular, has indeed become one of the new political concerns. the specific problem that we face is that of setting priorities within the area of capacity building, linked to the scaling-up of activities. the need for capacity building is accepted everywhere. there is interest in the issue and, in principle, there is a readiness to provide funds. however, when one takes a snapshot of what happens in practice in this area, one sees a patchwork of uncoordinated activities, usually very small-scale and attached to specific projects or programmes. if we really want to promote ownership, this situation has to change. what is needed is improved priority-setting by developing countries themselves and more coordination and harmonisation among donors to promote activities on a larger scale and over the longer term. in fact, the formula i keep using in addressing what needs to be done on capacity building is that instead of having it as an additional measure here and there in the actions we are implementing, we have to turn capacity building into hard-core, big-money development cooperation and not shy away also from agreeing to cover recurrent costs as part of the capacity building effort. finally, i agree with the criticism of the problems in assessing the impact of the community-financed interventions within our statistics in the commission. this problem is being addressed. it is one of many aspects of the same problem that we are working on. the communication will deal with this issue by devoting a chapter to current practices within the commission and lessons learned. the recommendations of the rapporteur in this respect are very welcome. i conclude by once again thanking the rapporteur.
(fi) mr president, if the world were a village of 100 people, 61 of its inhabitants would be asian, 13 african, 12 european, 8 north american and 6 south american. two or three villagers would have access to the internet. and the phone would be owned by 16 people. sixty-five percent of the world's villagers would have had at least some schooling at some point in their lives. every year, there would be a funeral in the village, and at least two new children would be born there. the world bank classifies as absolute poor those who live on less than a dollar a day. the measure is partly misleading because it does not take into account, for example, the importance of public or community services for the disadvantaged. nor does it say anything about health, powerlessness, or insecurity. so, in addition to wealth, we should also talk about power. in addition to the wealth accumulated by the few, attention should be given to the management of the common wealth, represented by, for example, common lands and territories, oceans, a large proportion of forests, biodiversity, the global climate system, public spaces in cities, public libraries, schools and hospitals, and scientific and traditional knowledge. the debate on globalization and poverty tends to focus on one dimension of poverty: income and consumption poverty. the arguments for and against globalisation are also often tied to economic definitions of poverty: proponents point to gdp growth, opponents point to food price increases and unemployment increases. the consumer culture and economic thinking have a paradox: the richer we are, the more we are in need, because globalisation is changing needs and the nature of poverty. many communities, with areas where natural resources have not been destroyed or exploited, with sufficient food and shelter, and where communal culture is healthy, can be at least as good places to live as cities in rich countries. however, as a result of globalization, many initially prosperous members of the local community become suffering members of the global community. it is therefore very good that mr ferrer's excellent report is strongly and on every page about the partnership. it recognises the mistakes and wrong attitudes in development policies that have prevailed for too long, such as the focus on technical solutions and the recourse to foreign models. it recognises the need for a participatory process and the views of local communities. i would also like to stress the right to our own natural resources. i can only hope that this will be taken seriously in the member states when allocating and targeting development cooperation resources. in my country, there was an election in march. during the election campaign, almost all parties promised to increase development funding. it was even included in the government's programme as a pious wish, but the actual implementation was left to the next government. this is not strengthening capacity, and in a globalising world, it will eventually take its toll on us.
(fi) mr president, tackling the education crisis is one of the most effective means available to break the cycle of poverty and achieve the 2015 human development goals. the committee on development and cooperation and rapporteur, mr van den berg, deserve our thanks for their report, which shows a sense of familiarity and genuine concern. now it seems that the target of universal primary education by 2015 is falling short. it is a single factor that is significantly undermining all the other work being done to reduce poverty in the world. the funding needed is €8 billion per year. it is a lot, but it is not impossible, for the right of everyone to education, as stated in the united nations declaration of human rights over 50 years ago, to be realized. the rapporteur rightly points out that the commission should participate in the world bank's 'education for all' 'fast track' programme. the rapporteur also warns the eu against accepting training as a service to the wto gats agreement. basic education must be kept free of the rules of free trade and the criteria of productivity. we must trust that raising the level of education and increasing literacy is the best that world trade can hope for. the g8 countries must therefore also be reminded of their commitment to substantially fund the fast track initiative. my own experience of working in the field of development cooperation confirms that there is a need to invest in girls' education in particular. it's the key to progress. it can be used to combat overpopulation, because an educated woman will give birth to fewer children. women's education is also a way out of the trap that many asian women and girls find themselves in: life-threatening from the womb to the grave, discrimination that begins before birth and continues throughout life. where the girl is a foreign asset and a burden on the family, only a determined change in attitudes and the opening up of educational opportunities will guarantee change.

effects are still manageable. the status quo is certainly not an option for european governments. to prepare for the overall and budgetary effects of population ageing, we need to increase employment, reduce public debt and reform pension and health care systems. the additional costs of postponing pension reforms will be borne by today's young people in the future. change - even necessary and inevitable change - always faces resistance to change, as we have also experienced this week in strasbourg. however, this resistance to change must not be used as an excuse and an obstacle to delaying the necessary reforms. the economic situation in europe is worrying and our economic growth is clearly below forecasts. the disappearance of geopolitical risks now gives some hope for faster economic growth, but many experts also point out that the forecasts for the last two years have always postponed the acceleration of economic growth to the next six months. our weak economic growth is largely due to home-made, made in europe. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the only positive aspect is the low rate of inflation, which has averaged around 2% and is likely to fall further. at their summits, the heads of state of our member states have pledged to implement structural reforms to promote growth and stability. however, the practical implementation has been very lacking and weak economic growth and mass unemployment are the prices we pay for postponing these reforms. if we want the broad specific measures needed in areas where internal political deadlock has prevented progress in certain member states. the eu continues to be characterised by the under-utilisation of human resources. benefit systems is needed, including by easing the excessive tax rates for low-income earners. however, tax relief must be granted to all wage earners. more flexible working time arrangements and lighter regulatory regimes would improve both labour market participation and employment opportunities.

(fi) mr president, the liberalisation of the energy market has been an ideological project. the smoke screen of market thinking has been spread over some real problems. markets are always one-way, they always operate in the here and now. the prices do not take into account the structural changes that occur when resources are depleted. the liberalisation of the gas market will not secure the financing of investments that will take place over decades. when all the additional energy needs of the eu are met by gas and when gas is imported from the east, those who need gas should contribute to the investment costs of new gas fields. in a liberalised market, only short-term benefits are discounted. it should be possible to conclude long-term gas supply contracts. in the short-term interest market, they don't make them. in the electricity market, ownership of networks may pose problems. if the public authorities do not invest in and maintain the transmission grids, the market will not function. without public investment in the grid and network responsibility, electricity cannot be moved from one place to another. i was working on the report on nuclear safety in the committee on industry, foreign trade, research and energy. in that capacity, i see the problems that may affect electricity pricing. nuclear power may be cheap on the spot market, but we are not sure that the prices fully take into account the costs of decommissioning and disposal of nuclear waste, which extend over hundreds and perhaps thousands of years. if these costs are not discounted to the market price of electricity sold today, competition will be distorted. market liberalisation will determine prices in the short term but will not solve the major problems of the future; new oil wars in iraq will arise. our group would have liked to secure public and public-interest energy services in the context of these liberalisation and privatisation directives.

right direction. if necessary, the commission will make further proposals to further liberalise the market. contrary to what a few of our socialist friends think they know, the nordic free and open market works well. consumer choice has increased, prices have fallen, service has improved and operations have become more efficient. the market mechanisms have worked perfectly. it must be accepted that in a free market, prices can sometimes rise. rarely, though, does one of the driest summers in a century coincide with one of the coldest winters. the key to liberalising the energy market is to guarantee unhindered access to the networks. the clear separation of network activities and the sufficiently harmonised role of regulatory authorities are therefore the two key elements of this package. without them, we can forget the word 'competition' when we talk about the electricity and gas markets. it is time to finish the package, the compromises proposed are entirely acceptable. the (fi) mr president, we in the committee on budgets have drawn attention to the fact that the use of the guarantee fund mechanism is a very cheap way for the eu to provide economic aid to third countries. the guarantees enable the countries in difficulty to obtain credit from the international market at lower market rates. the absence of credit losses for the eu in recent years is an indication that the eligibility criteria have worked. in recent years, the guarantee fund has provided financial assistance in the form of macrofinancial assistance and general loan guarantees to the european investment bank. the ceiling for their use is set by the guarantee fund ceiling laid down in the financial perspective. the committee on budgets has noted with surprise that the commission and the european investment bank did not make full use of the guarantee fund's capacity last year. the resources available have been squandered and poor countries have not been helped. the use of this assistance instrument may be supplemented by minor technical changes to the guarantee conditions which do not in practice cause difficulties for the union budget. all the institutional parties are prepared to reduce the financing rate of loans from the current 9% to 8%. this would reduce the amount of money to be covered by the corresponding transfer of the guarantee fund for each credit transaction. the general guarantee on loans granted by the european investment bank could also be reduced from 65% to 50% of the loan. this measure would have the same effect on the use of the guarantee fund as a reduction in the financing rate, and the same maximum quota would be allocated more than it should be. the commission shall make such proposals without delay.

mr president, the preparatory commission has made progress on many policy issues. it is particularly important that the fundamental rights of eu citizens are defined as a binding part of the union's action. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council by the end of the year. the task of the preparatory committee was to make proposals to make decision-making more efficient, clearer, more democratic and more transparent. this mandate cannot involve the creation of new institutions. the council's work certainly needs reform, decisions must be made in an open way and there must be continuity in the work, but this cannot be achieved by a permanent long-term president who is outside democratic control or by reducing the commission's initiative and executive powers. the support of citizens is particularly essential for the future of the eu. one institution that is visible in all the member states is the commission. in the interests of equality, therefore, all member states must continue to have a member of the commission. the work of the preparatory committee has been moving in the right direction in the development of eu legislation. i support the call made today by commission president prodi to abandon the right of veto in the eu decision-making process. the use of veto power will not do any good, because it will only complicate and delay decision-making. one of the eu's priorities during this period has been food safety. however, the location of the food authority has not been resolved because this veto has been exercised. it is necessary to open this knot in thessaloniki and decide that the permanent seat of the food safety authority is helsinki. the helsinki european council has already made all the preparations to enable the agency to start work immediately after the decision. finally, i would like to express my special thanks to the greek presidency for its contribution to maritime safety.

(fi) mr president, almost a hundred years of history have shown us europeans that in the face of our continent's difficulties, the united states has always been a helper in need. in world war i, it became the key to preventing the expansion of imperial germany. in world war ii, the united states was instrumental in freeing germany and france from hitler's yoke and the rest of europe from his quest for power. the end of the cold war, with the dissolution of the soviet union and the liberation of central europe from communism and the countries under soviet yoke, was in a crucial way the result of president ronald reagan's arms race, which the soviet economic and social system could not withstand. today, when we talk about the crisis in relations between the united states and europe, we are talking more about the crisis in relations within europe and the crisis in relations between certain individual countries and the united states. the majority of the eu's 25 current and future member states have normal relations with the united states. the unilateralism of some countries in the internal affairs of the eu shows that we are still light years away from a common foreign and security policy in times of crisis. the majority of the 25 european countries supported the un security council's approval of the launching of the iraq war. most do not accept the us claim to be able to be placed outside the international system on war crimes issues. we disagree on the meaning of the kyoto agreement and every day there are negotiations to resolve economic differences. nevertheless, the similarity of the basic values and the common security aspects, where europe is still more of a recipient than a donor, provide a solid basis for looking to the future in supporting the transatlantic partnership and indeed in building the programme of action that my colleague james elles has just mentioned. we in europe can no longer catch up with the united states in defense technology - perhaps we don't need to. but we can - and we must - settle our differences and work towards a common foreign and security policy. only then will we be a worthy partner in unifying matters and strong enough on matters where we disagree with the united states to have our views taken into account in the united states, whose largest trading partner we are - as we heard about us investment in europe. they show that the united states is interested in the positive development of our relations, that it is essential for us. we can accept a boycott of french wines in the united states if the counterpart is the ordering of 100 airbuses to the united states, as just happened about a week ago.

capability in the member states. i myself recently investigated the situation by sending inquiries to the relevant authorities in the baltic sea countries. the answers were stark: finland and sweden be insufficient even if they were together. for example, the gulf of finland is already transporting some 40 million tons of oil a year, and that amount is expected to double within ten years. the commission should initiate an inventory of oil-defence capabilities in eu waters and determine the minimum capacity required to meet regional conditions and transport volumes.

(fi) mr president, commissioner, allowances are not ready-made in nature and are not the result of human activity. they're administratively allocated abstract rights to pollute the atmosphere. emissions trading is not a market mechanism. it redistributes emissions among polluters, but does not reduce emissions. the most unfair way to allocate emission allowances is to auction them. it's as simple as a radio frequency auction: they bought and sold airspace and put all the operators in trouble. this must not happen to european industry. when the system only covers co2 emissions, the release of other pollutants into the atmosphere is free. there are many questions that the directive does not answer. let me list some of them. the emission allowances are traded on the stock exchange. as an economic instrument, should an emission allowance be treated as a share or as an option? how do you tax the sale or the non-sale? how are allowances recorded in company balance sheets? how are they treated in the personal income and wealth tax system? it is necessary to ask whether the eu has the competence to decide on all these issues in the same uniform way for all. emissions trading is done on exchanges, and there is always speculation involved in the exchange. there is a danger that the price of the emission allowance will be set by speculators whose activities are not linked to industry or other emission production. in that case, the industry of the member states may suffer from this mechanism proposed by the united states, in which the us industry is not involved and in which profits and losses are decisively dependent on the administrative first allocation of emission allowances. the emissions trading directive is a raw fruit that should have been ripened longer. there are many members of our group who do not believe in the ets. however, many members will vote in favour of the compromise amendments because they are better than parliament's report.
(fi) mr president, i would like to thank from the bottom of my heart all those who have been involved in the construction of this compromise on which we are voting tomorrow. it has been said here on several occasions that after the first reading it was hard to believe that we would get to this point, that we would have an emissions trading directive which is so sensible and rational in its content that it can be accepted from different points of view. we must remember that this is only one of many ways in which we are working to reduce greenhouse gases. if this method is not available, some other method should be used and it has been considered and shown to be a cost-effective system and in this sense it is good that it is introduced. but i would like to emphasize that these last-minute discussions and negotiations have resulted in the removal of the excessive amounts that seemed to be involved, such as the cap on national emission allowances. the fact that the auction is voluntary is also a very good thing. this way, the companies involved are not burdened. all in all, congratulations to the rapporteur and to all those involved. we still have a long way to go in achieving the kyoto targets, but i believe and hope that through these compromises that have brought about this report and this directive, we will be one step further.
(fi) mr president, it is difficult to imagine any other report that would have such an impact on european conditions and that policy-makers know so little about. europe is now entering uncharted territory. even theoretical research on the effects of emissions trading is only a name. when we received the commission's proposal a few years ago, it became clear that there were huge gaps in the proposal on which the european economy, competition and environmental strategy were to be built. there is no assessment of the effects of enlargement, no guidelines on the accounting status of emission allowances in companies, no information on vat, not even a strategy against serious market disturbances and speculative attacks, no precise information on member states' emissions, let alone those of companies, no presentation of the rights of appeal. it is unfortunate that we have received such an incomplete presentation of such a vital matter. we've done a lot of work on this, but whether we can pull it off remains to be seen. the trilogue solved the problem of the so-called trendline cap in a formulation proposed by the commission which allows the member state to approach the kyoto target along its own path. this will also encourage member states to adopt a more responsible transport policy in the light of the kyoto protocol, while providing a margin of manoeuvre. that's our real problem, you know? the trilogue solution on project mechanisms must also be supported. it is not worth setting stricter criteria than those agreed in marrakesh. instead, there is a need to establish eu rules on how to convert the emission credits from these project mechanisms into tradable allowances without compromising the climate change imperative. the emission allowances from the project mechanisms will also have an interesting impact on development cooperation. i believe that the interest of member states in development cooperation will increase strongly, because they see in projects opportunities to earn emission credits. a particular problem in the context of emissions trading was combined heat and power (chp) and process emissions. if the seasonal cycle related to the nature of chp is not taken into account, the emission reductions will also not be achieved. the trilogy now acknowledges and recognises the special nature of chp. member states may take into account the potential for reducing process emissions, which in practice means that the best european steel mills can obtain full allowances and do not have to bear extra costs in the highly competitive world steel market. that will reduce the risk of carbon leakage and increase the real opportunity to combat climate change.
(fi) mr president, the report on the negotiating procedure for the 2004 budget gives a good indication of parliament's priorities. i would like to thank my own group member and rapporteur-general, mr jan mulder, for a very good report. the next year's budget is legally difficult because for the first four months the eu is a community of 15 member states, but will be enlarged to 25 member states from the beginning of may. since each member state must be able to participate in the decision-making process concerning it, the budget of the enlarged union can only be decided after enlargement. but the principle of the budget's universality and annuality requires that it be drawn up immediately for the whole year. the solution sought, therefore, whereby the budget is actually dealt with in december but the new member states' shares are not decided until the supplementary budget for next may, is the appropriate procedure. the budget is also now clearly presented on an operational basis. this clarifies the operational expenditure, administrative expenditure and implementation responsibilities of each policy area separately. the reformation is excellent. it increases the information value of the budget and facilitates its monitoring. however, this system must be developed, inter alia, by fully incorporating administrative expenditure into the budget of each policy area, as the report points out. the main weakness of the preliminary draft budget is the scarcity of payment appropriations. the payments are only 0.99% of the member states' gross national income, the lowest level in 17 years. at the same time, the number of outstanding commitments has increased dramatically, reaching eur 126 billion. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competition' in the definition of the term 'competition'. this will require improving and simplifying governance at all levels. i wish my friend mr mulder every success in his future work.
(fi) mr president, the rapporteur for this year's budget, mr göran färm, has made a good report on the implementation of the budget and i would like to thank him for it. the report rightly touches on two key points, namely non-payment of commitments and the relaxation of commitments, the so-called n+2 rule. as i have already mentioned, the amount of commitments outstanding in april was eur 126 billion, an increase of more than eur 15 billion in one year. in the structural funds in particular, these rals amounted to eur 94 billion, i.e. almost three years' appropriations. this is bad. at the beginning of the current programming period, the so-called n+2 rule was adopted, which states that if no payment is made for two years, the commitment is void. the commission seems to interpret the rule in such a way that advance payments which are not based on any invoices submitted are considered as payments. this allows the project to be kept alive year after year with small pre-payments, even before the project has even started. the commission shall strengthen the monitoring of the n+2 rule and give it its original substance.
(fi) mr president, many of my colleagues have raised the idea of incorporating environmental objectives into public procurement criteria. however, this is entirely in line with the idea, also contained in the treaty, that environmental policy is integrated into all eu policies. parliament has done a good job of incorporating sustainable development into production legislation. however, this supply-side effect needs to be supported by the demand side as well and can be influenced by public procurement where the justification for including an environmental criterion in the call for tenders can be put forward. the so-called helsinki case, which the court of justice has decided on, highlights an important point. when the subscriber is a public body, it represents the public. in this case, not only must the public's economic interests be protected, but also, for example, the public's health must be protected. this is precisely what the helsinki case was about. it goes without saying that public health is also a long-term economic benefit whereby the public body saves at least as much as it could have saved by choosing the lowest bidder.

i suggest. mr president, commissioner lamy, ladies and gentlemen, it is a great honour for the italian presidency, but also a great responsibility, to speak before the european parliament on the subject of the international criminal court. it is certainly an honour because italy, from the very beginning, has supported the criminal court more than many other countries and is the country which gave its name to the rome statute. but at the same time it is our responsibility to continue to support, with the same determination as our predecessors, the efforts of the international community to make viable an international jurisdiction that will put an end to impunity for the perpetrators of the most abhorrent crimes. the court is proving a great success. to date, 90 states have become parties to the rome statute. the court held its inaugural session this year with the election of its president, judges, prosecutor and registrar. it is now a fully functional reality in international relations and the european union can be proud to have made a decisive contribution to this development. the italian presidency has an important legacy to defend and intends to defend it. a few days ago, on 16 june, the council reaffirmed its commitment to the court in a common position which has the political significance of reaffirming the european union's full support for this institution. the action plan of may 2002 will be updated on the basis of the common position. action in favour of the court will remain an essential component of the union's foreign policy action. it is well known that in this action the european union will have to face delicate and complex issues, but this will certainly not diminish our commitment. our initiative will go in several directions: first, italy will represent the european union at the second assembly of states parties in september. we are preparing for this meeting with great diligence and will spare no effort to resolve all the outstanding questions relating to the institution and its functioning. secondly, in order to ensure the effective functioning of the court and to guarantee its independence, the european union will continue to encourage states to transfer swiftly and in full their contributions, as determined in accordance with decisions taken by the assembly, and to accede to the agreement on the immunities and privileges of the court. thirdly, the european union will continue to call on those states which have not yet done so to become party to the statute, because we believe that the widest possible accession is desirable and necessary. in accordance with the common position, a wide-ranging awareness-raising campaign has already been launched in this respect, in particular in the context of political dialogue meetings with third states. fourthly, the european union will continue to defend the integrity of the rome statute, and this also applies to our relations with our friends in the united states of america. as you can imagine, this is a frequent topic on the agenda of the euro-atlantic dialogue and was raised, just last week, at the eu-us summit in washington. the european parliament has already expressed its views on the situation in the middle east. we share the same core values as the united states of america and attach the same importance - a great importance - to the objective of ending impunity for the most serious crimes and identifying personal responsibility in their commission. but objectively - we must admit - there are different sensitivities in the assessment of the court's role. the united states, in its imposing global projection, is concerned about the possibility of politically motivated charges against its citizens. the european union considers that the statute of the court of justice provides sufficient guarantees of impartiality and authority, such as the high quality of the judges and the principle of complementarity of the court's work, to alleviate, i would say to dispel, concerns about a possible instrumentalization of the court's activities. my predecessors have informed this parliament of the developments in this ongoing debate between the european union and the united states of america and of the initiatives being developed by both sides. i do not repeat what is known to everyone, but i would like to indicate to you how the italian side intends to proceed during the presidency. the united states fears, among other things, that the court may pose a threat, even indirect, to the conduct and implementation of un peacekeeping operations. the european union considers the court to be an important safeguard to protect peacekeepers as well and in this context the un security council has for two consecutive years considered the us proposals to exclude peacekeepers from the court's jurisdiction. in both years, the european union was able to express its firm support for the court at the security council meetings open to non-member states. this year, the union also publicly expressed its support for the convening of the security council meeting on 12 june. on that occasion, all members of the security council were asked to commit themselves to finding a solution which would preserve the integrity of the rome statute and the smooth conduct of peace operations: the commission has already made a number of proposals. the united states, as is well known, is pursuing the conclusion of a network of bilateral agreements to exempt its own citizens from the court's jurisdiction. yesterday we heard the news that the united states had suspended military assistance to certain countries, including five countries which are about to join the european union, which have declared themselves not to be party to such agreements. the union regrets this initiative. we reserve the right, of course, to go into it further in view of further contacts with washington. i would remind you that last september the council adopted conclusions setting out some guiding principles to be respected in the conclusion of exemption agreements in order to preserve the integrity of the rome statute. the conclusions and guiding principles were addressed primarily to the member states and the candidate countries, but they are clearly relevant to all states party to the rome statute. these documents have been circulated to a large number of third states in order to make known the position of principle which the european union has already adopted on the matter. the european union is firmly convinced that the international criminal court will make the world safer, or rather, will contribute to making the world safer, fairer and more peaceful. we believe that the mere concrete institution of the court will have a deterrent value against the commission of the most serious crimes. by ending impunity, the court will strengthen the rule of law and make a vital contribution to peace. in conclusion, the court will very soon prove to be a truly independent and effective institution. it will do so by working concretely, and those who are suspicious of it will be able to see - i repeat, in the concrete work of the court - that the rome statute guarantees the highest standards of justice and does not lend itself - or at least we must work because it never lends itself - to political exploitation. this will obviously be one of the commitments in the frank and friendly dialogue that the presidency will have with the united states of america.
commission. - mr moreno campo, recently appointed prosecutor of the international criminal court, said at his swearing-in ceremony: 'a fully independent and impartial international criminal court is a source of hope, but it also raises fears and misunderstandings.' this court is indeed the hope of a new era for international justice, it provides an effective tool to combat impunity and is therefore a deterrent - as the president of the council has just said - for those who would be tempted to commit serious criminal acts which this court has jurisdiction to try. but it also raises fears and misunderstandings, if we refer to the position of the united states. it is this fear of political persecution of american personnel that has led to efforts around the world to secure bilateral immunity agreements for american citizens and to the renewal of security council resolution 1422. to secure these agreements, the united states did not hesitate to threaten to refuse military aid to countries which did not sign them. what does this position mean? the united states, which is not a signatory to the rome statute, considers itself free to conclude immunity agreements, particularly because of the considerable number of american soldiers deployed around the world. whatever the value of this argument, it is certain that the important contribution of the united states to peacekeeping operations cannot be overlooked. it is also clear that the countries which have acceded to the rome statute cannot use a back door to evade their obligations to the international criminal court. the guiding principles adopted by the union in september 2002, to which the presidency of the council referred, are very clear on this point: we do not consider these agreements to be necessary, but we accept them provided they meet certain well-defined criteria. the agreements which the united states is seeking to conclude do not meet our conditions and are clearly intended to undermine the rome statute in that they do not contain any firm provisions to avoid impunity and extend impunity beyond the scope of us nationals present in a territory for certain military or strictly administrative reasons. how, then, do we make our position known? we did so, in particular, on the occasion of the common position of 16 june last, which states unequivocally that the guiding principles are not to be reduced to a kind of internal kitchen, but that they must be applied to justify the protection of the integrity of the rome statute in our relations with third countries. this position is the result of a number of approaches and dialogues. my colleagues patten and verheugen have joined forces with the greek presidency in sending joint letters to the foreign ministers of the acceding countries, the foreign ministers of the candidate countries and the foreign ministers of the balkan countries. they stressed the need to take account of the union's position. we have instructed our heads of delegation in third countries to stress the importance of this issue. in countries which have already signed an immunity agreement, our heads of delegation were asked to look more closely at the likelihood of ratification of these agreements. these diplomatic efforts build on the significant support we have given to ngos campaigning for an effective international criminal court through the european initiative for democracy and human rights. the european council in thessaloniki recently confirmed the union's full support for the international criminal court. what about the results? after recalling our principles and our action, let us be lucid. the results are rather mixed. even if i leave aside the agreement hastily signed by romania in august 2002, and even if the acceding and candidate countries have resisted us pressure to align themselves with the union's position of 16 june, it is clear that the situation in the balkans remains unclear: albania, bosnia and herzegovina and, most recently, the former yugoslav republic of macedonia have signed such agreements, none of which meet the conditions set out in the union's guiding principles. we have not hidden our disappointment and have communicated it to these countries. as for the renewal of security council resolution 1422, there is no doubt in our mind that the legality of this measure is, at best, questionable. we have allowed for an open debate in the security council where concerns about the legality of this proposal have been expressed. in a remarkable speech, the un secretary-general, kofi annan, not only expressed doubts about the advisability of basing this security council resolution on article 16 of the rome statute, but also questioned its necessity. the un peacekeepers remain under the jurisdiction of the state of which they are nationals and would be repatriated immediately if they had committed a serious crime. provided that the state of origin deals with the case properly, the complementary jurisdiction of the criminal court is not a possibility. finally, on behalf of the union, the greek presidency made it clear that there was no question of accepting an automatic renewal of this resolution in the years to come, thus preventing this process - since it is a process - from continuing unchallenged. finally, i would mention the substantial aid - 13 million euros, that is not a small amount - provided by the union since 1995 to the international criminal court through the european initiative for democracy and human rights, the awareness-raising campaigns we have carried out with parliamentarians, civil servants, civil society, judicial officials and even military personnel, for example, in the newly independent states. in addition, we are working on training defence lawyers to make this court a concrete and immediate reality. we are also considering how our support will take shape in the coming years, as we listen to the ngos and several meps who will speak at a seminar the commission will organise on 14 july to hear various views on this issue. i feel that i have defended and presented a line which is in full harmony with what the presidency of the council has just said. no one will be surprised. the international criminal court is certainly challenging the transatlantic relationship. as this distinguished team of 18 judges and prosecutors begins its work, we europeans are convinced that the international criminal court will justify our support and confidence. in doing so, it will also help to allay the fears that remain here and there in the minds of our belgian friends, the americans.
