(fi) mr president, the legislative package under discussion to create a single european airspace is very challenging. i would like to thank the rapporteurs at this stage for their good cooperation in taking this matter forward. the problem with europe's airspace is congestion. central europe is a huge air traffic. the smooth operation of this system requires cooperation between member states, and this has already been achieved. in a sparsely populated member state such as finland, this type of problem is not so much a problem. in particular, domestic air traffic can be handled with considerable flexibility on its own. the report's objectives are ambitious. there are a number of far-reaching proposals in the report. one of these concerns the division of airspace. the allocation of airspace to sectors could be carried out, where appropriate, by qualified majority. there are doubts as to whether this is necessary or sufficient for the member states to cooperate in order to achieve the objectives. it may well be that closer integration in the heart of the eu is necessary, but in the periphery, given the specific circumstances, it is unlikely to be necessary. there's also the problem of payments. if air traffic control services were billed on an average basis, it might well be that in some areas there would be a marked increase in charges. the more sensitive problem is the integration of military aviation and air defence into the european system. traditionally, military and defence matters are not covered by the co-decision procedure between the council and parliament in the eu, and there is no justification for this now. therefore, caution and respect for accepted principles should be exercised here too. but even in the face of these difficulties, both reports are very valuable and well-aimed.
(fi) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i too would like to thank the rapporteur, mr bradbourne, on behalf of my group, for the determination and the quality of the report he has produced. the reduction of congestion and the need to improve the functioning of the freight transport system, especially from the environmental point of view, are essential in the face of the increase in transport volumes. eu enlargement also brings new challenges in terms of transport routes. our group supports the marco polo programme and welcomes the council's common position. the eu can add value by supporting innovative and new technology projects. the aim must be to move from the most polluting modes of transport to others that are more environmentally friendly. however, no mode of transport should be excluded from the programme. this includes multi-modal transport, which may include road transport. i think it is important that alongside new activities there is the possibility of supporting the improvement of existing activities. in these cases, improvement can often be achieved with a more efficient cost-benefit ratio than by starting a completely new project. by concentrating only on new projects, the risk of distortions of competition in relation to legacy activities is obvious. the common position has approved the halving of the minimum thresholds for all actions. it also allows for the implementation of smaller joint innovative projects, for example by smes. the budget for the programme is quite modest and the objective is very ambitious. however, i think it is important that the regulation is adopted now and that the first projects are launched in the autumn. in the longer term, it is desirable that marco polo proves to be a viable option and receives additional funding, and that the possibility of increasing funding for important projects complementing the transeuropean transport network is explored.

(fi) mr president, the european union's regional and structural policy must be based on genuine solidarity between the different regions of the eu. the aim should be to achieve economic integration as well as to understand the differences arising from regional differences. in future proposals we must take full account of the needs of the new member states, ensuring that aid is used effectively to bring about structural reforms and that it has a lasting impact on the lives of citizens. while we take full account of the new member states, the regions in need of special support in the present member states must also be included in the new plans. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr pomés ruiz, for the very serious way in which he has put forward the idea that areas with persistent structural handicaps will continue to be eligible for special aid in the future. such permanent disadvantages include, for example, remoteness and geographical isolation. these areas are also sparsely populated and have difficult climatic conditions. it would be necessary to consider these permanent handicaps as a factor of competitiveness similar to that envisaged for island regions. it is therefore not important which instrument will be used to provide aid to the remote, sparsely populated northern regions, as long as these needs are adequately addressed in a spirit of solidarity and equality. i trust that the commission will, when it prepares a new proposal for structural and regional policy after 2007, take account in a fair way of the sparsely populated areas in the north and the distant regions.
the president, the commissioner! the report we are about to adopt contains a real fundamental change in regional policy. regions with persistent handicaps must be given new consideration in support policy. i think this fundamental reassessment is important. some disabilities are permanent. we know about such disadvantages in finland with glass buildings, and it was also stated in the important treaty of accession for finland, sweden and austria. however, it may also be mountain regions or island and archipelago conditions. i would like to see islands and archipelagos treated in a special way. when finland joined the eu, we found it difficult to maintain important support schemes. for example, certain general transport aids were examined very, very critically by the commission. we are not asking for large amounts of aid for sparsely populated areas, for the archipelago and for mountainous areas, but they can be crucial to the maintenance of the service. an important part of the policy may also be to give countries the possibility of certain minor public aids and to require undertakings maintaining a service of general interest to ensure that this service functions. we also need rules in the agricultural policy to enable small-scale farming in the archipelago to survive. it's extremely important to the archipelago as part of our world heritage, and we're trying to manage it as well as possible. the european convention proposes similar amendments, for which we are very grateful. finally, i would like to say that the concept of territorial cohesion was actually founded by the cpmr under its then president, stig östdahl. i am very pleased that parliament can contribute today to a new and important way of thinking which will help to make it possible to live and work in all parts of the union.
(fi) mr president, i would first of all like to congratulate the rapporteurs, mr mastorakis and mr pomés ruiz, on their excellent reports. cohesion policy is a fundamental condition for the existence and legitimacy of the eu as a whole. a mechanism is needed to boost prosperity in less favoured areas too. cohesion policy is based on the treaty on european union. both the current treaty and the new one being drafted by the preparatory commission present regional and social cohesion as a principle that permeates all policy areas. when new member states join the european union, they all need structural development. the risk is that funding for the old member states' regional and structural policies will be reduced. in this case, the economically weaker regions would have to pay more for enlargement than the economically stronger regions, which would benefit the most. therefore, funding for former assisted areas must not be reduced. in finland and sweden in particular, there are rare outlying areas, the current objective 1 regions, for which access to the market is difficult. these regions should therefore be considered as ultraperipheral regions, like the canary islands, madeira and the azores. the current inefficient and bureaucratic management of the structural funds is a particular problem. this is reflected in the under-utilisation of resources, which is catastrophically low. the budgeted money was not used in 2000 at eur 6.7 billion, the following year at eur 8.7 billion and in 2002 at eur 6.2 billion. the unpaid commitments currently amount to over eur 90 billion, or almost three years' budget. this is putting the credibility of the entire political establishment at risk. the administration needs to be simplified and reformed. the so-called n+2 rule, which was created to improve administrative efficiency, does not work because the commission accepts the payment of recurring advances even before the project has even started. that is why this regulation needs to be revised.
mr president, i am glad that mr pomés ruiz has noted in his report the special consideration and support needed for northern finland and sweden, where the population density is extremely low and the climate is harsh, and which are a long way from european markets. currently these areas have objective 1 status. no other parts of europe are as sparsely populated: they have an average of 4.4 inhabitants per square kilometre. eu regional policy has contributed to the development effort in these areas by improving competitiveness and encouraging entrepreneurship. the remoteness of peripheral areas causes high costs for marketing, trade, and transportation of goods and persons. these factors should be taken into account when determining sufficient support levels. as a result of the union's regional level of activities, northern finland and sweden have developed innovative solutions in the fields of elearning and ehealth, for example. northern finland can also act as an eu gateway to north-west russia, with access to vast national resources and hence huge potential. focusing cohesion policy on competitiveness by fostering economic development is welcome. however, it should not be forgotten that geographical handicaps often restrain development of the economy, production and hence employment. northern parts of finland and sweden are in unquestionable need of eu regional support and the structural disadvantages of extremely sparsely populated areas should be recognised independently of gdp. in addition to the sparsely populated areas, many other areas suffer from exceptionally high unemployment rates and these should also benefit from common initiatives. if they are not eligible under other structural funds they should benefit from the renewed objective 2 or other similar community initiatives. i am very happy to note that mr barnier has spoken about an a la carte model for the renewed objective 2. this is something which should be emphasised more in future. it is also very much welcomed by the parliament.

commission. the commission's directorate-general for development, and i myself, are aware of the clean development mechanism and the opportunities it offers. for developing countries, the cdm should be a good vehicle for the transfer of clean and modern technologies, which will contribute to their development efforts. however, the cdm is not a substitute for funding in the framework of development cooperation. firstly, the cdm is primarily a private sector or business sector instrument and public funding would be used only for enabling and capacity-building purposes or similar catalytic actions. secondly, even though the certified emission reductions that will be generated by a cdm project will have an additional value, that value may not be 'reportable' as official development assistance according to the reporting rules of the development assistance committee of the oecd. the commission is supportive of the opportunities that cdm may bring in terms of technology transfer, but is aware that not all developing countries will be equally interesting to the business sector in terms of investments. the commission has proposed an eu strategy and action plan for climate change in the context of development cooperation, in which support would be given to partner countries for cdm project preparation activities, including capacity-building. the communication on climate change in the context of development cooperation also explores the possibility of funding cdm projects in the category of least-developed countries. as regards guiding member states towards a common approach on cdm, we hope that the discussions in council on the commission’s communication will lead to agreement on an eu action plan, including actions related to cdm activities. i invite the european parliament also to give its opinion on the communication. assistance for the cdm activities included in the action plan may be funded from geographical budget lines on request from countries, and from the research budget. assistance can also be provided from the budget line 'environment in developing countries and tropical forests', for which cdm activities will be one of the priorities.

(fi) mr president, over the last ten years, access to clean drinking water has been the subject of several international conferences. we know that many things have failed, not least the commitment to provide clean drinking water for all by the year 2000. in ten years, the number of people without clean water increased by only half a billion, from about 1.7 billion today. one in four people in the world drink contaminated water. the situation forces decision-makers to seriously examine whether the means used were at all correct. universal, equitable and non-discriminatory access to clean water is a fundamental human right. water scarcity is a clear cause of the poverty cycle. experience has shown that the market cannot be left to solve the problem of water scarcity. clearly, the invisible hand of the market will not hand a glass of water to a thirsty person. it must also be recognised that privatisation generally raises the price of water and sanitation services, which naturally hit the poorest the hardest. since access to water is enshrined as a human right, it should affect the way we think about it. water resources should not be seen as the private property of any one country but as a common resource of the earth. however, it must be noted that treating water as a commodity to be given away free of charge reduces the chances of a well-functioning water supply system. the flexible practices advocated in the amendments for linking the public and private sectors are therefore necessary, provided that the human right to water is regarded as an inalienable principle. the lannoye report wisely emphasizes the link between water supply and sanitation and hygiene. about 6,000 people, mostly children, die each day from infectious diseases, often caused by poor hygiene. it would be a fundamental mistake to separate water, sanitation and hygiene. the first priority is to increase the domestic and international resources devoted to improving water and wastewater services in urban slums and shantytowns as well as in rural areas. the main challenge is to link sanitation and wastewater management to water supply, which cannot be achieved without significant additional investment by those responsible for water management. in addition, human-centred technologies for households to solve water and wastewater problems need to be developed. i would like to express my support for amendment no 19, which calls for the application of the debt-for-water swap so that debts can be written off and used to finance sewerage infrastructure.

what if they can't see a person through numbers? they're real people, not a popsicle. they have a pain threshold. i made the decision to go into politics because of these people. i often ask if it's anywhere and if it's enough, and i don't dare to answer. i think that if it does not become a practical decision, a gesture for the better, a hunger for justice with concrete consequences, i have no right to call myself a politician.

(fi) mr president, commissioner, the european union's growing energy needs will be met in the near future by natural gas. the gas reserves of the nearby regions, the north sea and north africa, will only be sufficient for ten or twenty years, and gas must be imported from the east. the pipeline route from the caspian sea region to western europe is a matter of great power politics. the eu's position on this issue must not be too dependent on nato. we have to protect our own interests first. it must be remembered that the largest gas reserves in the world are in russia. the best policy is that the eu participates in investments in russian gas resources against long-term supply contracts. the problems of liberalisation of the gas market are related to the fact that market participants on gas exchanges are only interested in buying and selling gas, but not in producing gas for investment. the market is too short-term and therefore measures by the member states to safeguard long-term supply contracts are also necessary. there's no such contracts in the market. you can't store gas the way you store oil. for this reason, the modes of transport and the needs for emergency storage of oil and gas differ. the responsibility for the security of supply of both oil and gas lies with the member states and cannot be delegated to market forces. our group supports the critical votes of the rapporteurs on the commission's proposals. we are well aware of the demands that karlsson's report should be returned to committee.

(fi) mr president, i thank the rapporteur for his report. if the activities of open source software, individual developers and small associations are used to make patenting more difficult by extending it, the foundations of the information society will be destroyed. innovations like linux that benefit everyone show how big things are - indeed, how much we are closing the digital divide. the copyright law, on the other hand, already protects the software code. the downside of software patents is that the designer may not always know that he is infringing the patents, but he may be subject to a huge liability. the limits of patentability must be defined in such a way that programs, algorithms or business methods cannot be patented as such and that patents cannot be used to prevent competition. we support small business in this sector.
(fi) in writing. the proposal for a directive on the patentability of computer-generated inventions has certainly aroused more passion than any other issue recently before parliament. my own position on the directive has been critical from the outset. however, i am not entirely opposed to the proposal for a directive, because i think it is good that the current confusing situation in the conditions for granting software patents is clarified. at present, with the lack of clarity of the rules, the decisions of the national patent offices and the epo may have been very different and therefore common european rules of the game may be necessary. however, the report will have to be amended a great deal before it can be adopted. the amendments i have tabled would make the directive less harmful to independent software developers and smes. my main arguments are as follows: the directive in its current form could seriously disadvantage independent software developers and european smes, which may find it impossible to survive the patent jungle of the big software giants. patent processing times are very long and applying for patents is expensive. they could therefore slow down and even prevent the development of a european software sector that depends on innovation and rapid reactions. in addition, communication and interoperability between software should be possible without high licensing costs. i'm all for open interfaces. algorithms, business models or computer programs as such should not be patentable. one of the main purposes of patenting has been to bring inventions to the attention of society. patents for computer-implemented inventions are almost useless as sources of information if the source code is not published. in the united states, for most software patents, the source code is published in the patent application as part of established business practice in the industry. this should also be possible in europe. i hope that tomorrow's vote will be a positive one and that as many of my colleagues as possible will show their support for the creative and innovative european software industry.

perspective. the commission's budget is structured as a 31 policy area activity-based budget. this has long been a clear objective of our group. it makes it easier to follow the eu's actions. the costs of each policy area are clearly visible. the administrative and operational costs are differentiated. the effectiveness of the action can be better assessed, as can the ratio of results achieved (fi) mr president, i would like to draw attention to the excellent report by mr jan andersson on adequate and sustainable pensions. it is good that parliament is actively involved in the debate on pensions, as pensions and their payment are one of the major social and economic challenges of the coming decades. ageing is no longer necessarily poverty, as statutory public pension schemes, complemented by private occupational pension schemes and private insurance, guarantee many europeans the ability to maintain their standard of living after retirement. however, the generation gap must be narrowed by a radical reform of pension systems, so that the burden of rising pensions is not placed on younger generations. four factors threaten the functioning of public pension systems without relevant reforms. these factors are the continuously increasing life expectancy, combined with low birth rates, high long-term unemployment and late entry into working life. large age groups will start to retire this decade, and by 2050, the number of pensioners in relation to working age will double. the share of publicly funded pension schemes is estimated to be as high as one fifth of gdp in some member states. i think that the european pension systems have largely achieved their social objectives. however, in order to strengthen pension security, the priority must be to raise the retirement age, to increase complementary forms of financing and to increase long-term private saving. at community level, we need coordination in the taxation of earnings-related pensions to facilitate the movement of people from one country to another and comprehensive internal market legislation for institutions providing earnings-related pensions. at european union level, we can help to defuse the pension bomb by helping to develop crossborder personal pension and savings schemes. i hope that this parliament will continue to act actively on these issues in the future, on the basis of this excellent report by mr andersson.

mr president, the constitution abolishes the present european union and establishes a new one which, according to the constitution, expresses the will of the citizens and the states. we know how to find out what the will of the nations is. it is represented by governments and national parliaments. the will of the people is not always the will of the state, and there is no alternative to a referendum to determine this. it would be fairer if it were to be organised in each member state separately but on the same day. if the draft constitution of the preparatory commission were to be put to the vote without amendments, i would vote against it and would urge others to do the same. the reason is that the preparatory committee worked in an undemocratic way. it was a dictatorship of the presidency, or politburo, and the preparatory commission did not vote on the more than six thousand amendments or outcomes put forward by the members. the preparatory commission implemented a redistribution of power in favour of the big countries and the union was militarized. the member states lose their right to a permanent member of the commission with voting rights and are no longer able to elect their own commissioners. all commissioners-designate must be committed to the european cause, but what is the european cause? it's a matter of federalism. the eu is being made into a federation, and the committee even adopted the constitution last night, contrary to the mandate of the thessaloniki summit, with the symbols of a federation, flags, anthems and national days. the constitution is militarizing the eu. it is a new military structural cooperation and a declaration annexed to the constitution by which the member states give each other collective security guarantees. the finnish army commander said that the adoption of the declaration would lead finland to nato, and that is not accepted by our people. these are the reasons for saying no to such a constitution.

(fi) mr president, the failure of the doha round interim review conference in cancun was a defeat for the acceleration of world economic growth. it was an undeserved loss for us in the eu too, but above all it was a loss for the developing countries. the atmosphere at the beginning of the meeting was cautiously optimistic. the european union and the united states had reached agreement on their joint proposal to reduce barriers to agricultural trade and subsidies, an agreement on access to generic medicines to improve access to medicines in developing countries had been signed - what went wrong? if we exclude the hasty decision of the president of the sitting, in my view, at a time when everyone had reserved at least 35 hours for effective negotiation, there must be many reasons. the new feature of the doha meeting was the presence of the so-called group of 21 countries. as has already been said here, this group may have had not only trade but also power policy objectives, including opposition to real or imagined us unilateralism. while these countries spoke as if they represented all the underdeveloped nations, they themselves were not prepared to give up their high barriers to trade with poorer nations. part of the cotton problem is connected to this. it is time to see more generally, not just in europe, that the development of the developing countries is beginning to diverge sharply, and it is good to ask the question of what is really a developing country. is it still india as a whole, or even china or brazil? other developed countries should also learn from the eu and conclude all-except-arms agreements, as we have done with the 49 poorest countries. it should also be remembered that the eu, in addition to being the largest donor of development aid, buys, among other things, 80% of africa's agricultural exports. formally, the meeting fell into the so-called four singapore issues. why, when every single country can make a residual reserve in respect of these? why, when it was agreed at doha that these were also on the agenda? it was more about the timetable. how much opposition to cotton trade liberalization contributed to the collapse of the convention is unclear, but it certainly was. a large number of ngos, as noted here, rejoiced at the failure of the meeting. it is to be hoped that the underdeveloped countries will not be caught in the future by the same kind of trap as in cancun. we have to move on. the eu and the commissioners negotiating on its behalf have nothing to be ashamed of or to hide. the commission did a great deal of preparatory work to ensure the success of the meeting and also used the negotiating power it had at its disposal during the meeting. the eu's task will be to strengthen the wto and accelerate negotiations, and thereby to make the eu's trade policy position even clearer to the less developed countries, which do not yet seem to know it.

adopted in 1998, is politically binding on the member states. the ultimate responsibility for arms exports lies with national governments. however, certain aspects of the arms trade are covered by the this approach. for this reason, the preparation of our proposal, which is now being examined by the council's bodies, has been delayed. we hope, however, that it will be adopted very soon. the european parliament's report and - as you recalled, mr von wogau - the very important proposals of general morillon very accurately highlight the link between export control measures and the development of a european union policy on defence equipment, as proposed in the commission's communication of march 2003. there is obviously a link between the relaxation of controls on the movement of arms within the european union, on the one hand, and the tightening and harmonisation of export control practices to third countries, on the other. in this connection, proposals for a common control system within the european union should be carefully examined. in the light of this idea, which you and i have worked on in the framework of the convention, the possibility of creating a european arms export control agency and, more generally, a european arms, equipment and research agency should be explored, which could be one of the tools of a single arms market, which, as you have recalled, is a real necessity. i would like to say this in response to what mr von wogau said, and thank you, ladies and gentlemen, for your attention.
(fi) mr president, mr von wogau's clear and balanced report deserves warm thanks. the report analyses the current situation and proposes a coherent follow-up. in particular, taking account of the practices must be reviewed and harmonised without delay and the eu's code of conduct must be enforced at the eu's new borders and in countries with which it has a stability and association agreement. for exports to less stable countries are missing. the remedy proposed in the report, namely greater transparency and multilateralism, is the only way to correct the shortcomings and prevent arms transfers that threaten human rights, regional stability or sustainable development. the development and harmonisation of legislation and the establishment of a register and licensing system for arms export transactions at eu level are essential, and the idea of establishing a european arms export control agency, as presented in the report, must be seriously considered as a coherent follow-up to control of defence procurement in finland is to prevent the circumvention of arms export bans imposed by the european union, the un security council and the organisation for security and cooperation in europe, the eu code of conduct on arms exports and other international agreements and commitments on export controls. it also aims to prevent the illicit manufacture and trade of arms. it is therefore to be expected that my own country will support the line presented by mr von wogau.

an important basis for reliably identifying negative emissions trends and creating a framework for reversing them. but it does not sufficiently meet the obligations of the kyoto protocol. the current reporting does not correspond in its breakdown and precision to the sectoral breakdown of the emissions trading directive adopted in the eu in the summer, for example. there have also been major shortcomings in the member states' activities, and the data not provided and incomplete data have had to be supplemented by calculations. the text now adopted will significantly improve the quality and accuracy of the reporting. at the same time, the intention is to rationalise the frequency of data transmission by separating annual and periodic reporting. in the so-called compromise package, it was agreed between the rapporteur and the shadow rapporteurs of parliament and the council that member states should establish national inventory systems to assess emissions of greenhouse gases by (fi) mr president, russia is already the most important of the union's neighbours: our common border of 1200 km will double in size as the union expands to include the baltic states. russia is still economically modest, with a gdp slightly above that of the netherlands, but its economic potential is arguably enormous. the union has already stepped up its energy cooperation with russia, as we heard the commissioner say, which is essential if we are to replace the depleted energy resources of the north sea and avoid becoming exclusively dependent on the energy resources of the middle east. but russia's vast forest resources, together with its precious and non-precious metal reserves, such as those in siberia, will also be one of the foundations of its rise in the coming decades, the other being its high level of science and expertise. the european people's party and the european democrats consider russia to be our most important neighbour now and in the future, whatever geographical explanation neighbourhood cooperation may have. we believe that the union must use all the economic and scientific means at its disposal to help russia to develop a stable market economy, to develop democracy and to establish the rule of law, since a different russia, still a nuclear power, would also be the greatest potential threat to the union's security. russia and the rest of europe are bound by fate. (fi) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, relations between the european union and russia have developed, but not sufficiently. it is worth remembering that almost 40% of russian exports go to the european union, 6% to the united states. it is now estimated that after enlargement imports will rise to 60%. at the same time, the union's dependence on russian resources is growing - as is likely to happen, for example, with natural gas in the coming decade. also, economic growth is currently the fastest in the world in russia. the russian president, who is campaigning for the presidency, has set two goals: first, to create a strong central government with principles of legality, and second, to ensure that economic growth is passed on to the vast majority of russia's poor. it is in the european union's interest that russia is able to solve major problems, ranging from widespread poverty to human rights, freedom of the media and the war in chechnya. we want to see that through economic, political and cultural cooperation we can also influence russia's problems. we've also tried to isolate russia. it was a gift to stalin, who put curtains on the windows and started to slaughter his own people. it is now important that we support russian civil society at its core. this is exactly what many russians want from us. we can't leave them alone! they estimate that the russian leadership often listens better to messages from the union than to themselves. it is for these purposes that russia should become a member of the wto as soon as possible. the partnership and cooperation agreement must be renewed to meet the needs of today. i would say that the elections in chechnya were problematic, as they have been in the past, and the community could not accept them. we propose an international conference and mediation on this matter, and also the rapid access of aid organizations.
(fi) mr president, the biggest single project between the eu and russia is the creation of a common european economic area. it has been difficult to put it into practice, as it was a bilateral project between the eu and russia. at the same time, both sides have developed their cooperation relations with other countries. russia has thus recently concluded an agreement with ukraine, belarus and kazakhstan on the establishment of a common economic area. the eu has its own separate contractual arrangements with these states. i think it would be wise to try to bring these cooperation arrangements together by forming a wider multilateral economic cooperation area, which would include not only the eu and russia but also ukraine, belarus and kazakhstan and other willing nic states. where appropriate, the model could be taken from the european economic area, which has been established between the eu and the efta countries. such an agreement would fit well into the eu's evolving broader european policy. the eu's new policy for a wider europe also offers opportunities for developing crossborder cooperation along the eu-russia border. the commission's proposal for a new financial instrument for neighbourhood cooperation will remove the problems we have had in coordinating internal and external financial systems. the russian authorities should be informed in good time of the new financing system in order to ensure its functioning from the outset. finally, i would like to congratulate the russian government on its efforts to ensure that human rights are respected in chechnya and to do all it can to find a peaceful solution to the problems in this region.
(fi) mr president, one of the great historical tensions of our time is the struggle between state sovereignty and universal human rights. the traditional power of the sovereign ruler is manifested above all in the right to decide over the lives and property of his subjects. even in the era of nation states, we have not been able to get rid of two persistent and profound violations of fundamental human rights, namely the death penalty and widespread torture. it is good that the european union, whose own external sovereignty is very weak, has focused on removing these two serious flaws in the core area of its foreign policy, namely human rights, especially now that the idea of armed security is dominating the formation of the new world order, and at the same time violence against its own citizens is becoming alarmingly proportional. a state that kills and tortures should never have international legitimacy. the question of the fate of the un system is the contradiction i mentioned at the outset. human rights have been considered a special strength of the world organization, but at the same time its charter is firmly anchored in the idea of sovereignty. it is thus at the intersection of two eras and two different political worldviews. i believe that the time is now ripe to take this step into the future and to make every effort to achieve a comprehensive moratorium on the death penalty throughout the world as an intermediate step towards its abolition once and for all. there are diplomatic and other risks involved in this project, but the goal is worth taking.

(fi) mr president, madam commissioner, i regret that i cannot really defend my report on support for transeuropean networks in the cultural sector. my key proposals narrowly lost the committee vote. i hope that parliament will be able to correct the situation in the vote tomorrow, especially since the committee's proposal to continue the so-called earmarking in the funding of cultural organisations is, according to the opinion of parliament's legal service, clearly contrary to the financial regulation. i am shocked that there is a strong tendency in the committee on budgets to act contrary to the financial regulation. until now, i have on many occasions defended to the citizens the action of the union as being based on the law and not on the stronger law. now i don't know what to say. the commission's proposal for a community action programme to support cultural organisations has been born of necessity, that is to say, it has been made necessary by the transition to an activity-based budget. european cultural organisations previously funded under category a of administrative expenditure need a legal basis to continue their funding. the change gives us a unique opportunity to create an action programme for the transeuropean networks in the cultural sector, defining the criteria for funding and organising the possibility of networks receiving eu support as defined in title vi of the financial regulation. it emphasises, among other things, the principles of transparency and equal treatment. the commission proposal covers not only the cultural organisations operating at european level, which were previously financed under budget heading a-3042, but also the european office for minority languages and the mercator network centres, which were financed under budget heading a-3015, and the grants under budget heading a-3035 for the protection of nazi concentration camps as historical monuments. in this context, it should be stressed that the problems raised in the report do not concern them, but that the most important thing for the financing of the office for minority languages and the concentration camp memorials is that the legal basis should be in place from the beginning of the year. we talked about the urgency here, but i think that if we want to, we have a chance to get results quickly. the disagreements over the funding of cultural organisations relate to three points. firstly, there is a contrast between the application procedure and the earmark made by parliament. the second is the length of the programme. since the unanimous decision of the cultural council, as far as i am aware, is absolutely in favour of extending the programme to 2006 instead of 2008, i have tabled an amendment to that effect. i think it is a very good idea to include the funding of cultural organisations in the new cultural programme which is being prepared. the third problem concerns the degressivity of the aid rates. i also disagree with the majority of the committee on that. a small gradual reduction is acceptable in my view, so that room can also be made for new organizations. we must bear in mind, among other things, the enlargement of the eu. the amendments i tabled also include a proposal to allow the list decided on by the committee on budgets to be implemented next year, due to time constraints. this ensures the necessary continuity. the 2005 application procedure could then be launched in good time at the beginning of next year. i also propose the possibility of multiannual funding and, among other things, a safeguarding addition to the funding criteria for european orchestras, proposals which were also rejected in the committee's vote, although they are clearly in the interests of cultural organisations. the commission's handling of the proposal so far has been a rich experience in many ways, but not in all of them a stimulating one. in defending the open call, i wanted to change the current practice, which is partly arbitrary and haphazard, into one based on open and clear rules. even now, our list contains organizations which do not really exist any more. the division of labour between the institutions must also be borne in mind: the commission makes proposals and implements the will of the legislator. parliament is the legislator and i do not think it should try to exercise executive power. when the draft eu constitution makes parliament the primary legislator, parliament itself must value that role.

durable good such as the car is one of the most difficult to move within the union, is completely unsustainable. the considerable obstacles to the transfer of a car from one member state to another are still due to the differences in tax legislation and inflexible administrative practices. in the field of motor vehicle taxation, citizens of the union are in a completely unequal position depending on their country of residence. the commission's proposal to abolish the registration tax could be a solution to most tax problems and should be implemented as soon as possible. it should be noted, however, that the european parliament has previously supported the commission's proposals for the elimination of double taxation, but the council has still not adopted them. the council should seriously consider what is ultimately in the interests of the citizens of the union in these matters. secondly, the focus of the taxation of motor vehicles must shift away from ownership. in many northern member states, such as finland, the purchase of a new ordinary family car is shockingly expensive, due to the registration tax. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the road network is not affected by the accident. for this reason, and also for reasons of road safety, the taxation of cars should rather be shifted to taxation of use. but the taxation of use must also be reasonable and the overall tax burden on motor vehicles must not be too great. this is particularly important in sparsely populated areas such as the north, finland and sweden, where the car is often the only means of transport available, with very limited public transport. for the same reason, i am also not in favour of abolishing the preferential tax treatment for diesel fuel. the removal of the diesel tax advantage would impose significant costs on the peripheral regions of the union, which are already suffering from long distances, and the latest studies show that diesel cars emit significantly less co2 than petrol cars. this was also referred to by mr schmidt of the eldr group. however, emissions from passenger cars are a significant source of greenhouse gas emissions and therefore it is necessary to act in such a way that, where possible, consumers are guided to choose products that are environmentally sustainable and cost-effective. for example, the taxation of fuels should take account of environmental friendliness by reducing the taxation of such fuels. in the case of taxation of cars, environmental friendliness can also be taken into account either in the form of reductions in potential user charges or tax relief for eco-friendly accessories. i also welcome mrs honeyball's proposal for tax relief for safety equipment. this promotion of safe, environmentally (fi) mr president, i share the concern expressed in mrs dybkjær's excellent report about the underrepresentation of women in the european parliament. it is already a sad fact that, as i look around, there are very few women here, especially young women with families. as many have pointed out, the situation seems even more alarming in the light of the statistics for next year's post-enlargement elections. the percentage of women in the national parliaments of the new member states is at best no more than 30%, and in more than half of the new member states the proportion of women in parliament is closer to 10%. it is important to remember that there is room for improvement in the present member states, including my own country, finland, which can nevertheless be considered one of the pioneers in the field of equality between women and men. why not vote for women when more than half of the voters are women? the report identifies constituency parties and electoral mechanisms as the only ways in which women are underrepresented. systems such as the finnish electoral system have guaranteed equal representation of women and men. here in the european parliament, almost half of the finnish delegation is female, so i think there is nothing wrong with the system. instead of long and hierarchical party lists, the finnish electoral system votes for both the candidate and the party, and in practice the european parliament elections have largely been elections of individuals, in which candidates are given priority over parties. under these circumstances, women have done well. of course, traditions and electoral culture also have their effect. but this shows that women will be voted in if they run for office. but why are young women in particular not interested in politics? why are women not being made more involved in the exercise of power? these are questions that i think also give rise to self-examination. why not the eu? women often find eu decision-making too distant, abstract and high-flying. women are the heroes of the day, eager to get down to the nitty-gritty. working conditions are often difficult, especially for women with families. i myself have noticed, as i am expecting my first child, how difficult it has become to combine motherhood and the career of a member of parliament. the eu's maternity rights regulations are directly oppressive to young women. maternity leave is not well known, and in parliament, for example, it is equated with sick leave. there is a lot of room for improvement here and this situation is reflected in the employment situation in europe as a whole. women are not sufficiently encouraged to pursue careers and start families, which leads many women to deliberately delay having children. the message the eu is sending must not be one of career or motherhood.

commission. madam president, on 27 october 2003, after more than six years of negotiation, the council adopted the energy tax directive. that directive includes some aspects of the commercial diesel proposal and notably it gives member states the possibility to apply differential tax rates to commercial diesel and non-commercial diesel. however, the energy tax directive is still based on the principle of minimum rates only. hence, it does not remedy the problem of distortion of competition on road haulage markets, which stems from the significant differences in diesel taxation in the member states. only harmonisation, or at least a greater approximation, of commercial diesel taxation will put an end to those distortions of competition. three parliamentary committees, the committee on industry, external trade, research and energy, the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy, and the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism have broadly supported the commission's proposals for that reason. however, the debates in the committee on economic and monetary affairs have also shown that there is wide recognition of the existence of such distortions. however, that committee does not share the views of the commission on the need and the means to reduce those distortions of competition. i recall that, according to article 93 of the treaty, provisions for the harmonisation of legislation concerning excise duties must be adopted to the extent that such harmonisation is necessary to ensure the establishment and proper functioning of the internal market. it is clear that the distortions of competition resulting from the very significant differences in excise duties applied to diesel by the member states are a threat to the functioning of the internal market. i would like to point out that the commission reacted positively to many amendments contemplated by the rapporteur of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and other members of parliament. in particular, the commission could in principle have accepted the following three items. firstly, the creation of an indexed fluctuation band for excise duties applicable to commercial diesel. such a fluctuation band could be considered a 'second best' if a harmonised rate for commercial diesel cannot be achieved. the second item is the alignment of the present proposal with the terms of the energy tax directive, in particular as far as the definition of commercial diesel and tax rates are concerned. third, there is the issue of the implementation of a refund system, as a practical tool for differentiating duties for commercial and non-commercial diesel. to my surprise, it appeared that these amendments were subsequently not considered by the committee on economic and monetary affairs to be sufficient to allow support for the commission's proposal. finally, the council, according to the minutes concerning the adoption of the energy tax directive, is clearly committed to pursuing its work on the commercial diesel proposal. for the reasons i have given, the commission is still convinced of the merits of this proposal. i would invite members of parliament to consider their position on this issue very carefully before the vote on it this week.
the rapporteur. (fi) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i regret to say that i cannot agree with the commission's view that the time is ripe for this proposal to achieve full harmonisation of excise duties on diesel for professional use. my criticism is mainly directed at the following points: first of all, the justification for the proposal: is there really a problem? the commission has two main reasons for presenting its proposal: environmental protection and fair competition in the internal market. although i am a firm supporter of both aspects, i have my doubts as to whether the proposed proposal will have the desired effect. first, environmental impacts should be considered in a broader context. the environmental benefits of the proposal are questionable. the commission claims - citing evidence that can best be described as anecdotal - that trucks make extra runs to fill their large tanks in countries where excise duty and thus the price of fuel sold at the service station is lower. the proposal refers to a 1997 oecd study which showed that 20% of fuel sales in switzerland, for example, were linked to this type of 'bensaturism'. even if this figure were accepted, it is impossible to know whether it is a real substitution, i.e. that truck drivers do not fill their tanks before leaving the country of origin, or whether this is due to drivers making a detour to fill their fuel tanks in certain countries. i wonder whether this is really such a big problem that it merits such far-reaching action, complete harmonization, as the commission proposes. i am not at all convinced that it is even desirable to eliminate the differences in excise duties between the member states. i think that tax competition is very healthy, especially since the proposed attempt at harmonisation would lead to higher taxes in most member states. finally, as a person from a remote region of the union, i believe that regional aspects must also be taken into account. bensaturism is unlikely to affect scandinavian transport in the same way as it is said to affect german or belgian transport in the congested areas of the union. i think that the proposal has not been put forward in the right context. although it frequently refers to the ongoing dialogue on the new transport policy and to proposals for the introduction of infrastructure charges, there has been no real discussion of the interaction between the two initiatives. the commission has put forward concrete proposals on infrastructure charges and taxes, so it would have been better if these measures had been proposed simultaneously. otherwise, there is a clear risk of imposing disproportionate burdens on the transport sector, both in the form of higher diesel taxes and new infrastructure charges. although it is true that article 8e of the proposal refers to this problem, it applies only during the transitional period and only to goods vehicles weighing less than 16 tonnes. moreover, how would this proposal affect the new member states, some of which currently apply much lower taxes than those in the eu today? is it realistic to expect, as the commission proposes, that the harmonised tax rate will be applied to all 25 member states by 2012? i fear that substantial price increases in several of the new member states would impose such enormous economic burdens and increase inflation at a time when they are seeking to reduce their inflation levels in order to join emu that this development is not politically possible. moreover, the candidate countries have already done a great deal to adapt to the current minimum tax rate, and the relatively small increase envisaged in the energy tax directive may already cause difficulties for some countries. one reason why the commission is putting forward this rather complex proposal is to bring the tax rate on passenger cars quickly into line with that on unleaded petrol. the commission justifies this tax treatment on the basis of environmental considerations. it is necessary to consider the impact of the harmonisation of these rates on, for example, research and development in the field of diesel engines. it should also be borne in mind that european manufacturers are currently the market leaders in diesel technology in a competitive environment. the impact on the oil industry and refineries will also have to be considered, as the proposal is likely to reduce diesel consumption. finally, i would like to say that before the summer break i tried to amend this proposal so that it could be adopted by parliament. however, i have received only verbal assurances from the commission that this change, which parliament hopes for, will indeed be what the commission is pushing for in the council. it is very unsatisfactory that in the consultation procedure parliament has to either reject the proposal or give the commission a blank mandate in negotiations with the council. i therefore hope that in future the new treaty will allow for the co-decision procedure and qualified majority voting in tax matters, which will make it much easier for parliament to accept the commission's proposal with certain amendments and to come back to the matter at second reading.
the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection has prepared an opinion. (fi) mr president, europe must intervene in the automotive sector if we are to meet our kyoto commitments. it would be short-sighted to pursue the kyoto targets at the expense of those who create jobs, unless we first make full use of the opportunities for reducing emissions from transport. the eu has already concluded agreements with european car manufacturers, for example, to produce cars that consume less fuel and pollute less. the directive under consideration is important in targeting the same demand side, by making the costs visible to consumers. it is a pity that the commission's proposal is so isolated. in order to be accepted, it should be seen in a broad context: within a decade, a pan-european tax system, including vehicle registration and user charges, a smart infrastructure pricing system and the harmonisation of fuel taxes, should be achieved. since such a comprehensive approach cannot be guaranteed at present and since this directive fixes the level of fuel taxation for a rather long period, it does not seem that a decision will be taken. the overall approach would serve consumers and ensure a balanced development of transport conditions in areas with low population density and no public transport. in rural and remote areas, people are more dependent on private cars and the external costs of transport are lower than in other areas. people tend to reassess their choices only when there are major changes, such as a change of residence or job or the purchase of a car, and significant developments in public services may not be perceived. therefore, large changes in directly observable costs, such as fuel prices or road pricing, are important triggers in the selection processes. in this sense, a linear model for raising fuel excise duties may be too flat and slow to enable consumers to make real discoveries that change their consumption habits. in any event, it is important to emphasise publicly from the outset the ultimate objective of including the external costs of transport in prices. diesel cars do consume less fuel and produce less carbon dioxide emissions, but they do have a lot of benefits. however, it must be remembered that diesel cars produce more nitrogen oxides and particulate matter emissions, which pose a serious public health risk. the harmonization of the excise duty rates for diesel and petrol can therefore be considered a legitimate objective.

(fi) mr president, mr president of the council, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the security of information systems is a major concern and a topic of discussion in modern and vulnerable societies. electricity grids, water supply, traffic control, hospitals, all of these can be disrupted at any time, anywhere. this fall's bastel virus was perhaps the first to have business behind the destruction it caused. because hackers or crackers are the auxiliaries of the shady business, mostly porn, i guess. stealing sensitive information from ordinary people is also, of course, a crime when you think about it. the many violations of people's privacy can occur on a truly industrial scale. you gotta take it. the development of the online society is being held back by its increasing insecurity. last year, 75% of european companies had no information security strategy at all. less than 2% of it investment is invested in information security. in two out of three companies, information security is still not considered a strategic issue. the data protection act is due to enter into force in the member states in october. it means that no unsolicited e-mail may be sent without the express consent of the recipient. spam already accounts for over half of all email, for many more than 90 percent, as it does for many of us in this room. the credibility of e-mail and its reliability are now being destroyed. we voted on this in this chamber last year, when almost half of us didn't think spam was a problem. they objected to this prior authorisation. now that's not gonna cut it. even the operators are now being forced to clean up the content. this raises problems of freedom of personal communication. security requirements are increasing very rapidly as networks and communications interlock our lives. at the end of the broadband, we're constantly "open-minded". wireless internet is a direct prey to the intruder. the damage will be to all people: individuals, businesses and society. the safety problem is compounded by the need for many components and subsystems to work together in a complex environment. the human factor is also a risk. so data security is everyone's business, everywhere. information security is a political issue and therefore we must solve it. and that is why we are here, on an exceptionally fast timetable, to create this agency for europe, the network and information security agency. without security, the lisbon objectives will not be achieved. the lack of information security would lead us into an era of piracy. triple measures are in progress. we have laws in place regarding electronic communications and data protection. the commission is preparing principles for the prosecution of cybercrime and means of improving information security, and this is the case with enisa. i thank my colleagues, the council and the commission for the very good cooperation. it shows how we have now understood this situation. the agency will be a centre of excellence for coordination, analysis and advice. it will have very close cooperation with the public and private sectors. the agency shall contribute to the assessment of risks and the development of management methods. international cooperation is essential. the agency should be located in a location where state-of-the-art information and communication systems, including wireless, are already in place and functioning. it must have world-class centres of excellence and the best european experts within easy reach. as rapporteur, i have worked towards a compromise that would bring all member states on the board. at the same time, it does not interfere with the day-to-day work of the manager, the staff, the board and the working groups. it is true that a large board is a necessary forum for cooperation and a guiding factor. the voting rules are a little problematic. we can go back to them in a few years. the agency serves member states with very different levels of data security. the role of the companies in our compromise is strong, thanks to mr van velzen, because they have most of the networks and expertise. i also want to thank erika mann, who has also been driving this thing hard. the compromise package has been adopted by the committee, the council and the commission. i expect us to feel our responsibility and vote in favour of this tomorrow. it is leading to greater security, employment and development.

i suggest. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the first action plan for the northern dimension of the european union's external and crossborder policies 2000-2003, adopted by the feira european council on 19 and 20 june 2000, will expire at the end of this year and intensive preparatory work has therefore already been started a year ago to continue the policies relating to the northern dimension beyond 2003. in particular, i recall that the guidelines for a new action plan were established at the ministerial conference on the northern dimension and the arctic window in greenland in august 2002 and at the subsequent meeting of foreign ministers in luxembourg in october of the same year. in line with these guidelines, the commission has therefore started contacts with all interested parties, including regional organisations and associations, as well as representatives of the private sector, with a view to drawing up a working document on the new action plan. on 11 june this year, the commission presented to the council the working document on the second northern dimension action plan for 2004-06, which takes up several proposals put forward by the parties concerned and takes account of the experience gained with the first action plan, as well as of the new needs for the coming years. the document was then examined by the council on 29 september 2003 and subsequently adopted at the european council in brussels on 16 and 17 october 2003. on that occasion, the heads of state or government underlined that the northern dimension will become increasingly important in the context of the enlargement of the union and will have to make a significant contribution to the development of the union's new neighbourhood policy in the whole region. the 2004-06 action plan sets out the guidelines for the next three years. it ensures, in this respect, the continuation of the 'northern dimension' of the union's policies, defined in the document as a 'horizontal concept applicable within the existing financial and institutional framework in the region concerned'. the northern dimension is of particular importance for certain priority areas of the union where the potential for added value is greatest, such as the economy, business, infrastructure, human resources, education, culture, scientific research, health, the environment, nuclear safety, natural resources, crossborder cooperation and regional development, and justice and home affairs. the common framework provided by the northern dimension for the promotion of political dialogue and practical cooperation will cover a wide and diverse geographical area around the baltic sea and the north atlantic. particular attention will be paid to regions with specific needs, such as kaliningrad and the arctic region, and therefore it will be essential to strengthen, in the context of the northern dimension, interaction at all levels between russia and the european union. the commission will continue to play a leading role, but all stakeholders, whether at national, regional or local level, or in business or civil society in general, will be called upon to play an active part. concrete activities will have to be carried out in the five major priority areas already mentioned - economy and infrastructure; education, culture, research and health; environment, nuclear safety and natural resources; crossborder cooperation and regional development; justice and home affairs - in a spirit of cooperation, based on participation open to all, subsidiarity and complementarity, an efficient allocation of tasks and comprehensive coordination and control.
the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on budgets. the northern dimension of the european union's external relations and crossborder activities is an important area of commission activity, and this will become even more important with the forthcoming enlargement. the commission is continuing to be active in the field of the northern dimension. in parallel with the implementation of the measures of the first northern dimension action plan, the commission played a key role in the preparation of the second northern dimension action plan. this second action plan covers the period 2004-06. following the preparatory work carried out by the danish presidency last year, the commission carried out an open and inclusive consultation process involving the main partners during the first half of 2003. this process has proven to be very helpful. the commission also took account of the european parliament's resolution of 16 january 2003. in june 2003, the commission submitted its proposals for the second action plan. following discussion in the council, the final version of the document was adopted by the council and endorsed by the european council at its meeting in brussels on 16 and 17 october 2003. the main objective of the second action plan is, in our mutual interest, to provide an instrument for regional cooperation with our neighbour, the russian federation. the northern dimension also aims to address the opportunities and challenges that could arise from the forthcoming enlargement. this means that it will help to promote stability and prosperity in this region, both within and outside the european union's borders. the northern dimension also makes a direct contribution to the objectives we have just discussed in the context of the debate on wider europe and the new neighbourhood. finally, the new action plan will contribute to the sustainable development of the arctic region and the indigenous peoples living there. the commission also continued its intensive work on the implementation of the first action plan. it's supposed to be done by the end of the year. in this connection, some recent results are worth mentioning. the commission also continued to support the implementation of the first projects under the northern dimension environmental partnership, the signing of the agreement on the multilateral nuclear and environmental programme in the russian federation and the development of crossborder cooperation in the context of the preparation of the first neighbourhood programmes following the commission's communication of july 2003. we should not forget the large number of projects already completed or still in progress and the concrete initiatives of the commission in the framework of community programmes which also contribute to the realization of the northern dimension. a full overview covering all sectors of the first action plan will be provided in the 2003 annual report on progress in the implementation of the northern dimension action plan. the commission will publish this report in the coming weeks. finally, we should also take into account the significant contribution made by the other actors, such as the member states, regional organisations or local or regional authorities. the northern dimension can only be a success if it is actively supported by all the actors involved in the region. this is an aspect to which we have drawn particular attention in the second action plan. the commission will of course continue to keep parliament regularly informed on the implementation of the initiative and we hope for parliament's active participation in the monitoring and review initiatives that will take place in the near future.
