commission. mr president, eight months have passed since i had the pleasure of addressing you on the commission’s proposal for a directive establishing quality and safety requirements for human tissues and cells. since that occasion in april 2003 there has been a considerable amount of effort, discussion and negotiation on the part of parliament and the council in order to arrive at the compromise before you today. i am fully aware of the tremendous work that these institutions have undertaken with the commission over the recent months. let me specifically express my appreciation to the rapporteur and to the members of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy for all their hard work on this important proposal. the compromise package will ensure that the increasing number of patients in europe who are treated with human tissues and cells can trust that these substances are not only safe but also of good quality. it constitutes another step forward in the regulatory framework on substances of human origin under article 152 that we initiated a couple of years ago with the blood directive. in my view, the main concerns of the european parliament have been successfully addressed in the compromise – and that is very much down to your rapporteur. let me now turn to some of the key concerns. i appreciate the difficult issues the environment committee had to address – in particular regarding ethics and other related issues. the commission is willing to accept the compromise position that strikes a balance between parliament’s wish to 'ensure' voluntary and unpaid donations, and the current proposal, which 'encourages' such donations. this amendment would introduce the wording 'endeavours to ensure' voluntary and unpaid donations and the idea of compensation in specific cases, leaving the responsibility of defining such compensation to the member states themselves. i must, however, make it clear that this compromise solution goes to the very limit of what the commission believes to be legally acceptable, given the restrictions of the treaty. the council of europe and the european group on ethics have stressed the principle of not-for-profit procurement of tissues and cells. the directive should therefore accord with this principle. however, i must draw a distinction between the act of procurement, to which the 'not-for-profit' principle should apply, and the secondary activities, such as the further processing, manipulation, testing, or the manufacturing of products, to which it should not apply. for example, whilst it might be considered unacceptable to take heart valves from a deceased donor and sell them, heart valves need to be treated to ensure the absence of infectious agents and preserved so that they can be stored for a longer period. these and other processes that have served to improve the quality and usefulness of the heart valve should not be constrained by the 'not-for-profit' principle. this point has been clarified in the compromise package. some amendments refer to prohibition of the use of 'cells derived from cloned embryos' for 'transplantation' – so-called 'therapeutic cloning'. such applications remain highly controversial with no consistent opinion amongst the member states as to either their ethical legitimacy or their safety. i would add that there is no likelihood of any such opinion emerging in the near future. we therefore consider it necessary to refrain from interference regarding decisions to be made by individual member states on the use or non-use of any specific type of human cells or tissues. i believe the best solution is to set a community framework on safety and quality requirements with sufficient scope to cover the use of such cells in those member states where it is permitted. the compromise package reinforces the thrust of the commission proposal in this respect. anonymity is another important issue. it is generally accepted that tissue and cell donation should respect the anonymity of both donors and recipients. this principle aims first to respect the confidentiality of the donor and the recipient, and second, to encourage altruistic donations. the common position introduces the possibility for member states to maintain or adopt different legislation in exceptional circumstances, such as the right of a child to know the genetic parents in case of the donation of gametes. i can accept further clarification of the rights of member states to make exceptions. finally, a word on organs. while the quality and safety of organs is indeed important, priority at this stage should be given to addressing the serious shortages of organs and the prevention of organ trafficking. the commission wishes to reflect on how it could best address the concerns of parliament in this sensitive area. we need first to get the overall framework right before proposing specific solutions. to this end, the commission is willing to present a declaration as follows: 'the important differences between organ transplantation and the use of other human substances such as blood, tissues and cells mean that a specific approach for organs in order to ensure safety and quality is necessary. such an approach in the current situation characterised by shortages of organs has to balance two factors: the need for organ transplantation, which is usually a matter of life and death, with the need to ensure high standards of quality and safety. the commission believes that before considering any proposal it is necessary to conduct a thorough scientific evaluation of the situation regarding organ transplantation. the commission will present a report on the conclusions of the analysis it undertakes as soon as possible.' to conclude, i look forward to your endorsement of the orientation presented in the compromise package. i believe that if parliament and the council can endorse the amendments, so too can the commission. i will be very disappointed if we cannot reach agreement. discussions on the 70 amendments would be very complicated, and i very much doubt whether we would arrive at a better solution than that offered by this compromise package.
(fi) mr president, i am here to defend the position of parliament at first reading. we wanted to ensure that the voluntary and free donation of cells and tissues is protected and that the trade in unmodified cells and tissues is prohibited, while at the same time safeguarding the industry. the council wanted to overturn parliament's position on the non-payment, citing the issue as being outside the eu's competence as a so-called ethical issue. i think it would be a big mistake to exclude ethical issues from eu decision-making. especially when we're talking about a community that likes to call itself a community of values. second, ethical questions rarely arise alone in a moral vacuum. for example, they are ethical precisely because they have an impact on human health, as in this case. we are now coming to a key policy issue where the work of parliament under the leadership of mr liese is of the utmost importance. an italian film director - if i am to return to italian films once more in this chamber in honour of italy's outgoing presidency - has stated that the restriction of the viewing angle is already an ethical choice. ethical issues cannot be dismissed, for such a choice is itself an ethical choice. as a philosopher by training, i have to say this. we also have a responsibility for how the rest of the world copies our practices. if we allow the trade in human body parts, we must take into account the fact that the developing countries are following the european example. such commercialisation will lead to exploitation, but also to increased risks. the line taken by the council also risks being inconsistent. several european union member states have signed a bioethics convention that explicitly prohibits the commercial exploitation of the human body and its parts. the rapporteur's conclusion is well founded. the legal basis for the directive is article 152 of the treaty on european union, which deals with health issues, and all the so-called ethical issues dealt with by the european parliament also relate to the protection of the health of the donor and the recipient. all donations made under suspicious circumstances, such as under financial pressure, also pose a risk to the recipient of the cells and tissues. many members of the council of ministers also agree with this assessment.

consider these three points in some detail: the introduction of toll systems should avoid a situation where the increase in private transport completely overrides the benefit of reducing commercial external costs of transport in such a way that, even for a given road toll, the marginal cost of transport corresponds to the marginal price charged. in this case, the transport costs are not the current external costs but the costs of the new balancing price for the volume of transport. the objective should therefore be to ensure that the volume of traffic on a charged route or in a charged area at different times is optimal and that the level of that optimal volume is determined by market mechanisms. in my view, these conditions are best met by systems based on satellite positioning, (fi) mr president, commissioner, i too would like to thank mr glantea on behalf of my group for the excellent report he has produced. he's done a lot of work on this, listened to all sides, and got a good result. i am a strong supporter of combined heat and power as an environmental and energy policy instrument for increasing energy efficiency and security of supply. as mr paasilinna has already said, we have good experiences in finland with co-production. the drafting of a union-wide directive is a difficult process, because of the technical requirements of the directive and the wide variation in the circumstances and practices of the member states. our group supports the compromises drawn up by the rapporteur and hopes that the directive will now move forward rapidly. i think it is important that we make progress on this matter, even if it is a small step. the eu is highly dependent on energy imports, which creates uncertainty and increases the risk of energy supply disruption. it is necessary to increase the share of co-production. this directive will create a framework for increasing co-production. member states must adopt commonly defined criteria to achieve the binding targets. experience has shown that if the objectives are not binding on the member states, progress is slow. the potential of combined heat and power can be optimally exploited when the various operators are aware of the technical possibilities available and find compatible systems. this is why cooperation and exchange of information on good practice between different actors is particularly important.
(fi) mr president, this has been discussed in many forums and i thank the rapporteur for his great contribution to this matter, which is essential to the functioning of the internal market and to the production of electricity and heat, and which i hope will now be concluded. the most recent compromise amendments have indeed made progress in the right direction. it is good that national objectives are not set, but that the approach is positive and encouraging. chp should be promoted where it is an efficient and natural way to generate electricity and heat. it is also important that chp aid is not limited to installations with an installed capacity of less than 50 mw. we must oppose the proposed support for small chp, for example, through free access to the grid and guarantee prices paid to small chp. this would distort competition in the open electricity markets that are currently being attempted to be created within the eu. small chp must be supported by other means, on a voluntary basis, without distorting competition and where chp is a cost-effective technology. biomass should be used more and more as a fuel for chp. this would contribute to the achievement of the targets for the use of renewable energy sources and the reduction of emissions. the effect of the ets negotiations may be to render special support for cogeneration of electricity and heat useless. in any event, the price of energy is expected to rise on the market as a result of emissions trading, to the detriment of users. it is important to promote the joint production of electricity and heat, as this is a good solution both economically and environmentally. the compromise proposals now agreed are a step forward in this respect.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i want to take this opportunity to talk about girls' education, because without girls' education, there is no equality for women. mr martens also mentioned that the unicef report 'the state of the world's children 2004' published last week is actually a frightening read. we make promise after promise, like in the millennium declaration. in the context of the un special session on children, we took action to reduce the gap between girls and boys in terms of illiteracy and to give girls good access to education. we have had the fast track initiatives in the world bank development committee, but what is happening? today, we know that more than 120 million children are out of school. fifteen percent of all boys and 21 percent of all girls are out of school. the situation is particularly acute in south-east asia and sub-saharan africa. under the world bank's fast track initiative, developed countries approved a $326 million financing plan last year, but so far only $207 million has been allocated. this means a funding gap of almost 118 million in one and a half years. we have not kept our promises. it is we in the developed countries who have not done our part. the developing countries have presented their plans and have done what they should have done. of the eu countries, italy and germany contribute only 0.3 percent and 2.1 percent of their development aid. only the netherlands has really lived up to its promises to the children of the world. what will happen after all the declarations and especially after the un special session on children, in which young people themselves were involved and demanded that they should have the right to education? yet, these are small sums compared with many other promises. i believe that the eu and the commission together should step up and live up to their promises, for example in the world bank.
the rapporteur. {\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\ the background to this report is the appeal of a young english woman two years ago, and i am pleased to see louise mcvay sitting in the visitor's gallery right now, watching where her determination and courage led. louise learned that she had ms and was bitterly disappointed that she was not given much of the medication that would improve her quality of life. in his country, as elsewhere in europe, people are even unequally placed in terms of access to medication and rehabilitation, depending on where they live. louise wrote to the president of the european parliament, who forwarded her letter to the committee on petitions. there are over 400 000 people in the european union with ms, and i myself was one of them when i started working in parliament and in the committee on petitions this spring, just as louis's petition was being considered. i was asked to draw up this report, which aims to clarify concrete ways of improving the lives of people with ms across the union. ms develops in young people whose lives are dominated by education, careers and family life. two out of three of the people who have it are women. the report shows the wide variation in diagnosis and treatment across the european union. this inequality in access to the best possible medication and therapy violates the fundamental right of citizens to health care. i believe that with better cooperation and the full support of all the institutions of the union, above all and obviously parliament, the ministers of health will be able to concentrate resources more effectively to support people with this disease. i believe that the capricious and unpredictable nature of the disease should be taken into account, so that those who have it are not so vulnerable to the loss of their jobs and their social environment. ms is an incurable disease, and the diagnosis does not merely require a person to adjust mentally to a change in his life. this disease puts both the sufferer and his family to a severe test, including financial ones. in view of this situation, i have tabled amendment no 3 at the request of louise mcvay. because of the wide range of symptoms, he was not considered to meet the requirements for medical services. the report proposes a number of measures relating to international cooperation and research and calls on the commission to contribute to their implementation. the report calls for the next meeting of the health ministers of the member states to include these wishes on the agenda, and i hope that parliament will be informed of the decisions and proposals for action. i have also tabled two amendments relating to the need to apply the precautionary principle, particularly in the handling of toxic chemicals. the question came up when tbt was suspected of being linked to ms in scotland. in any event, it is known that the substance in question has an effect on the central nervous system. although my report focuses specifically on ms and its specific features, there are a number of other similar diseases and after discussion with my colleague i have decided to make an oral amendment to add a mention of these. in my own country, finland, there are only 6,000 people with ms, but each of us has many colleagues among the hundreds of thousands of victims in the eu. those of us who are eligible for the new high-priced drugs feel a sense of community with all those whose illness has progressed to the disabling stage. we can fight more to improve conditions for all. i hope that this first report will be read carefully by those responsible for health care in the member states. after all the political and constitutional debates this week, parliament has the opportunity to show that it is listening to the citizen, not just to the president of the council. it is good that people believe that parliament exists for human wishes and for human requests for help and that it is not just a bunch of big buildings in an asphalt jungle in a few central european countries. one of the demands of the citizens and of my report seems to have been met, when commissioner diamantopoulou announced at the beginning of december that the commission was going to propose a specific directive to guarantee the rights of disabled people. mr president, commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. i am not one of those who usually thank the speaker without any reason, but now there is a real reason to say thank you. it is a particular pleasure for me, who has had the privilege of knowing mrs aaltonen for a long time, to congratulate her on her first report here in the european parliament. she is a positive person who has fought many important political battles for youth, women and now the disabled. it is a privilege for the european parliament to have access to her knowledge, energy and experience. these characteristics have been very useful in the preparation of the report. i am also grateful that mr aaltonen has taken account of my proposal on the genetic questions. pentti tienari, associate professor of experimental neurology in finland, has been researching this, and unfortunately, there are genetic factors behind it. the vikings are suspected of spreading the disease, since it is found primarily in the orkney and shetland islands and also in the area around kyro river in finland. the disease is also found wherever the germanic peoples have moved. i hope that this genetic factor will also be taken into account in future research. i would also like to say that the eldr group, which is usually very careful about the question of subsidiarity, will for the most part approve all these amendments. it is said that the member states should cooperate, and i think it is important that the member states cooperate in finding the binding standards. perhaps this can be done outside the eu structure, for example through cooperation between ministers within the council of europe. that's where we should be making binding health care recommendations. we should have a precise division of responsibilities between the eu and the member states.

(fi) mr president, i thank everyone for their speeches and support. i would like to inform those who thought that this responsibility belongs to the member states alone that i have been in contact with the ministers of health of all the member states and in a way they have already committed themselves in a very different way to this report after having been involved in its preparation.
mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. i thank the commissioner and mr harbour for the reports they have produced here, which i appreciate. it is a little ironic, however, that the best information on what happened at the summit has been found on the website of the radical party. the eu's own websites have contained very little information on this summit. what kind of information society are we living in? it is good that a summit was held, because international cooperation is needed in the field of network security. we think we have achieved something against spam by adopting the directive on privacy and electronic communications, but we have not got anywhere. as i said, international cooperation is needed. it was good that the final document contained statutes on technological neutrality, free competition, values and practices which have shown that europe is succeeding in creating an information society. the action plan and the final document contain, as is customary at international conferences, many good promises on how this should be developed and how we should be able to help the developing countries. countless international summits have now taken place, where we have made promises, such as education for all, and the fast track initiative within the world bank to help children and especially girls to get an education. despite this, nothing happens. as regards the fast track initiative of the world bank, the developed countries have not even been able to live up to their modest pledge of just over 300 million dollars. already after a year and a half, we're more than $100 million behind. when will the developing countries stop believing what is said in these international contexts? the liberals declared before this summit that we could not accept tunisia as the host country for the follow-up conference as long as freedom of expression was being violated there. in that respect, we are disappointed with the outcome.
(fi) mr president, mr commissioner, the development of information technology has been the greatest advance in the world in the last decade. it has completely revolutionized the way we manage, transfer and use information. whereas in the past it took thousands of pages of printed paper and extensive libraries and archives to store information, now the same amount is stored on internet sites and computer chips. in the past, the transfer of information required travel to the archives, copying of documents and mailing. now the same material can be viewed from a personal computer in your office. whereas in the past it took a few minutes to send a message by post and wait for it to arrive, today e-mail is sent in seconds to all parts of the world. there are no limits to the development of knowledge. the eu has seen this in many ways, including through the information society for all programme. the eu's development strategy to create the most competitive knowledge-based economy on our continent is therefore right on track. however, this development has a few limitations. the development of a balanced information society cannot be achieved by market forces alone. the development and construction of infrastructure must involve the community. it must ensure that the poor, young people and those facing exclusion are also able to use these services. at regional level, the networks must also cover sparsely populated and marginal areas. the eu's structural funds must be used. at the same time, monopolies must be broken. internet connections must be made affordable by opening up the last kilometre of the fibre to genuine competition. the cartels and monopolies in software and applications must also be prevented. the abuses themselves are a problem. the sending of spam and other deliberate disruptions must be made punishable. the development of the information society offers enormous opportunities for promoting both mental and material well-being. therefore, more attention must be given to this work.
the rapporteur. (fi) mr president, i would like to make an oral amendment by changing the words 'ms' in paragraphs 4, 9, 11, 13 and 15 to 'ms and other similar diseases'.
